L49: ## I. Executive Summary
L50: ## 1. Overview
L51: ## 1.1. Summary of Regulatory Action
L52: The Applicant, Novartis Pharmaceuticals Corporation, submitted NDA 218436 for remibrutinib (tradename: RHAPSIDO), an oral inhibitor of Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK), for "the treatment of chronic spontaneous urticaria (CSU) in adult patients who remain symptomatic despite H1-antihistamine treatment" at a proposed dosage of 25 mg orally twice daily. Because the Applicant redeemed a priority review voucher, the NDA was reviewed by a multidisciplinary review team on a priority review timeline. Each team recommends approval of remibrutinib for the proposed dosage and indication.
L53: To support the NDA for remibrutinib, the Applicant submitted results from two adequate and well-controlled clinical investigations, CLOU064A2301 (REMIX-1) and CLOU064A2302 (REMIX-2), hereafter referred to as Trials A2301 and A2302. Trials A2301 and A2302 were replicate phase 3, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trials that enrolled 925 adults to evaluate remibrutinib 25 mg orally twice daily over 52 weeks. Both trials demonstrated statistically and clinically significant improvements in patient-reported outcomes (PROs) of itch and hives.
L54: The available safety data were adequate for review and the observed safety profile is acceptable for its intended use in antihistamine-refractory CSU patients. The most common adverse reactions were nasopharyngitis, bleeding (petechiae, contusion, ecchymosis), headache, nausea, and abdominal pain. Bleeding reactions were mild or moderate in severity, and a Warning and Precaution for bleeding risk was added to labeling.
L55: The submitted clinical program is adequate to support the efficacy and safety of remibrutinib at the approved recommended dosage for the treatment of CSU in adult patients who remain symptomatic despite H1-antihistamine treatment. The clinical review team concludes that the improvement in itch and hives scores observed in the pivotal trials are clinically meaningful treatment benefits of remibrutinib that outweigh the potential risks and that remibrutinib will provide a novel oral treatment option in the armamentarium of CSU therapies. The NDA meets all applicable chemistry, manufacturing, and controls standards to support the purported identity, strength, quality, and purity of the remibrutinib drug product. The NDA includes appropriate preapproval nonclinical and clinical pharmacology studies. Postmarketing requirement (PMR) studies will be issued for pediatric studies to evaluate remibrutinib in children 12 to 17 years of age and 6 to 11 years of age with CSU, including a nonclinical juvenile toxicity study in rats, a pregnancy exposure registry, a pregnancy claims database retrospective cohort study, and a clinical lactation study.
L56: The regulatory action for this application is Approval.
L57: ## 1.2. Conclusions on Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L58: Substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) was established with two or more adequate and well-controlled clinical investigations.
L59: To support the proposed indication for remibrutinib, the Applicant completed two replicate, adequate and well-controlled, pivotal trials, A2301 (REMIX-1) and A2302 (REMIX-2). The trials evaluated the efficacy and safety of remibrutinib in 912 adult subjects with CSU who remained inadequately controlled despite treatment with second-generation H1-antihistamines. The study design consisted of a 52-week treatment period, including a 24-week double-blind, placebo-controlled treatment phase followed by a 28-week open-label period where all subjects received remibrutinib, and concluded with a 4-week treatment-free follow-up period. A total of 925 subjects were randomized in a 2:1 ratio to remibrutinib 25 mg twice daily or placebo across both trials, with 470 subjects in Trial A2301 and 455 subjects in Trial A2302. Efficacy results were based on the 912 subjects who were treated in the 24-week blinded, controlled treatment period. The coprimary efficacy endpoints were the absolute change from baseline in weekly itch severity score (ISS7) and weekly hives severity score (HSS7) at Week 12. The ISS7 (range 0 to 21) was defined as the sum of the daily itch severity scores (range 0 to 3) recorded over a 7-day period. The HSS7 (range 0 to 21) was defined as the sum of the daily hive severity scores (range 0 to 3) recorded over a 7-day period. The key secondary endpoint was absolute change from baseline in weekly urticaria activity score (UAS7) at Week 12. The UAS7 (range 0 to 42) was a composite of the ISS7 and HSS7. Higher scores indicate greater symptom severity with a score of 0 reflecting absence of symptoms. Efficacy outcomes based on the UAS7 and its ISS7 and HSS7 components are direct, relevant measures of how patients with CSU feel and are established PRO endpoints for CSU development programs.
L60: In Trial A2301, remibrutinib demonstrated statistically significant superiority over placebo with treatment differences of -2.63 (95% CI: -3.70–-1.56; p&lt;0.001) for ISS7 and -3.61 (95% CI: -4.85, -2.36; p&lt;0.001) for HSS7 at Week 12. Trial A2302 showed similar results with treatment differences of -3.23 (95% CI: -4.29, -2.16; p&lt;0.001) for ISS7 and -4.47 (95% CI: -5.71, -3.23; p&lt;0.001) for HSS7 at Week 12. Likewise, the change from baseline in UAS7 at Week 12 in Trials A2301 and A2302 were -6.22 and -7.68, respectively, compared to placebo and both statistically significant.
L61: Efficacy was further supported by statistically significant and clinically meaningful results of secondary endpoints assessed by responder analyses on the UAS7, including UAS7 \le 6 and UAS7 = 0. Compared to placebo, a higher proportion of subjects treated with remibrutinib achieved UAS7 \le 6 at Week 12 (50% versus 25% in A2301; 47% versus 20% in A2302) and complete absence of itch and hives (UAS7 = 0) at Week 12 (31% versus 11% in A2301; 28% versus 7% in A2302). Further, the onset of statistically significant differences in the treatment effect were observed at Week 2 with 34% and 30% of subjects in the remibrutinib group achieving UAS7 \le 6 compared to 3% and 6% of subjects in the placebo group in Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively.
L62: ## 2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L63: ## 2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
L64: **Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework**
L65: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of condition	CSU is defined by the presence of spontaneous, recurrent urticaria (i.e., hives) for at least 6 weeks duration and without an identifiable cause.
The main clinical feature of CSU is hives with prominent pruritus; some patients experience angioedema in addition to hives.
CSU is a common condition with peak incidence in middle age (4th to 5th decade) and higher prevalence in women (2-3x more than men).
Activation of mast cells and basophils is central to the pathogenesis of CSU.
CSU is a nonprogressive, self-limited disorder in most patients, with a 1-year spontaneous remission rate of 30 to 50% and an average duration of 2 to 5 years.
CSU is a common condition, primarily affecting adults, that causes significant morbidity in patients with diminished quality of life.
CSU is generally self-limited, resolving over several years, but some patients have more persistent disease.

Current treatment options	Current guideline recommended first-line treatment options for CSU include H1-antihistamines (H1AH) at approved doses and up to 4-fold higher than approved doses. Most patients are successfully treated with H1AH.
For adults and adolescents 12 years and older who remain symptomatic despite H1AH treatment, omalizumab is recommended by national and international treatment guidelines. Dupilumab was also recently approved for CSU patients who remain symptomatic despite H1AH treatment.
Approximately 25 to 33% of patients remain symptomatic despite treatment with H1AH and omalizumab. Available data show that dupilumab is not effective in omalizumab refractory patients. Off-label therapies (e.g., cyclosporine, H2AH hydroxychloroquine, leukotriene receptor agonists) may be used to treat refractory symptoms and systemic corticosteroids may also be used periodically to gain short term control of worsening symptoms. The alternative off-label therapies have significant adverse event profiles.
Although safe and effective therapies exist for treatment of CSU, a subset of patients experience symptoms refractory to available treatment options.
Additional treatment options with different mechanisms of action and routes of administration are desirable.
L66: ## NDA 218436
## RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
L67: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Benefit	Substantial evidence of effectiveness of remibrutinib in CSU patients who remain symptomatic despite H1-antihistamine treatment was established in Trials A2301 and A2302, two identical, phase 3, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trials.
The coprimary endpoints of change from baseline relative to placebo in ISS7 and HSS7 at Week 12 were statistically significant in both trials. Efficacy was supported by secondary endpoint results for change from baseline in UAS7 at Week 12, proportion of subjects with UAS7 \le6 at Weeks 2 and 12, and proportion of subjects with a complete response, defined as UAS7 =0, at Week 12.
Exploratory subgroup analyses of subjects who had previously used omalizumab showed a similar treatment effect as in the overall population.
Remibrutinib demonstrated statistically significant and clinically meaningful effects on the primary symptoms of CSU, itch and hives.
A meaningful treatment difference was observed by Week 2 and sustained through Week 24.
L68: ## NDA 218436
## RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
L69: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Risk and risk management	The NDA safety database includes approximately 2900 subjects who were exposed to at least one dose of remibrutinib. Trials A2301 and A2302 provide the primary safety database for the proposed dosage and indication, and consists of 912 total subjects, with 868 treated with remibrutinib, of whom 772 were exposed for ≥24 weeks and 438 exposed for ≥52 weeks.
There were no deaths in the clinical development program. Nonfatal serious adverse events (SAEs) and adverse events leading to treatment discontinuation were infrequent.
The most common treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were nasopharyngitis, hemorrhage, headache, nausea, and abdominal pain.
Remibrutinib treatment was associated with an increased risk of bleeding, likely due to its pharmacologic effect on platelet function. Bleeding reactions primarily manifested as petechiae, contusion, ecchymosis, or purpura. A small number of subjects discontinued treatment due to bleeding reactions; however, all reactions were considered mild or moderate in severity. There was no correlation between increased risk of bleeding and thrombocytopenia.
Occurrence of other adverse events of special interest (AESIs) such as infections, cytopenias, and hepatotoxicity was similar between treatment groups.
A dedicated hepatic impairment (HI) study revealed 2- to 3.5-fold increases in remibrutinib systemic exposures across any degree of liver dysfunction.
Remibrutinib is susceptible to CYP3A4-mediated drug interactions. Moderate and strong CYP3A4 inhibitors increase exposure by 2- to 4-fold, while strong CYP3A4 inducers decrease exposure by ~75%.
Due to its mechanism of action, remibrutinib has the potential to impact the immune response to vaccinations. Although the Applicant conducted a vaccine immune response biomarker clinical study, the design and endpoints were inadequate to yield clinically relevant results.
The safety database is adequate to assess the safety of remibrutinib at the proposed dosage for the proposed context of use.
A Warning and Precaution will be added for risk of bleeding.
Because a single nominal dose and dosing regimen of remibrutinib (i.e., 25 mg BID) was evaluated in the pivotal phase 3 trials, there are limited clinical data to guide dose adjustments for patients who are expected to experience higher systemic exposures with the recommended dosage. Given the benefit/risk assessment of remibrutinib for CSU, a conservative approach will be taken in labeling to recommend avoidance of use in patients with any degree of HI and with concomitant strong or moderate CYP3A inhibitors.
Given the lack of clinical outcome data, a Warning and Precaution will be added to recommend avoiding live or live-attenuated vaccines in patients receiving remibrutinib (b) (4) (b) (4)
The potential risks of remibrutinib can be adequately addressed through labeling and pharmacovigilance. No REMS is required.
L70: Abbreviations: BID, twice daily; CSU, chronic spontaneous urticaria; CYP, cytochrome P450; H2AH, H2 antihistamines; HSS7, weekly hives severity score; ISS7, weekly itch severity score; NDA, new drug application; REMS, risk evaluation and mitigation strategies; UAS7, weekly urticaria activity score
L71: ## 2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L72: As described in Section 1.2, the Applicant has demonstrated substantial evidence of effectiveness for remibrutinib 25 mg twice daily (BID) for the treatment of CSU in adult patients who remain symptomatic despite H1-antihistamine treatment. The safety assessment of remibrutinib revealed no concerning safety signals that would preclude approval or require narrowing of the indicated target population or adding a limitation of use. Potential risks can be addressed through labeling and routine pharmacovigilance. Given that CSU is generally a self-limited condition with several therapeutic treatment options, the team recommends adding a Warning and Precaution to the United States Prescribing Information (USPI) for risk of bleeding and avoiding use in patients with hepatic impairment or concomitant cytochrome P450 (CYP)3A4 medication use due to increased drug exposure and risk of adverse reactions. The Pediatric Research Equity Act PMRs will be issued to conduct studies in pediatric subjects 12 to 17 years of age and subsequently 6 to 11 years of age, pending results of a repeat juvenile toxicity study in rats. As CSU disproportionately affects women of child-bearing age, PMRs will also be issued to identify unexpected serious risks of adverse maternal, fetal, and infant outcomes resulting from remibrutinib use during pregnancy and unexpected serious risks of the potential presence of remibrutinib in human breast milk resulting in effects on the breastfed infant.
L73: In conclusion, the review team has determined that the benefit-risk assessment is favorable and supports the approval of remibrutinib 25 mg orally twice daily for the proposed indication of treatment of CSU in adult patients who remain symptomatic despite H1-antihistamine treatment. Although the development program did not enrich for a population of subjects who were refractory to all available therapies, there is a need for additional treatment options with varying mechanisms of action and alternative routes of administration.
L74: ## II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
L75: ## 3. Introduction
L76: Chronic spontaneous urticaria, formerly known as chronic idiopathic urticaria, is a nonprogressive, typically self-limited, condition characterized by recurrent pruritic hives (wheals), with or without angioedema, for 6 weeks or longer without an identifiable etiology. The urticarial lesions are typified by central swelling with surrounding erythema, pruritus, and evanescent time course with individual lesions lasting up to 24 hours without residual scarring or bruising of the skin. When present, angioedema manifests as episodic submucosal or subcutaneous swelling, often affecting areas of the body with loose connective tissue in an asymmetric pattern. CSU is distinct from urticaria and/or angioedema that occurs secondary to a known trigger or underlying disease such as chronic inducible urticaria, hereditary angioedema, mastocytosis, autoimmune conditions, etc. CSU is a common condition affecting approximately 0.5 to 1% of the general population with higher prevalence in adult females and peak incidence in the 4th decade of life. CSU is a self-limited condition in most patients with a mean duration of 3 to 5 years and 2-year spontaneous remission rate of 30 to 50%; however, some patients may experience symptoms for decades.
L77: The underlying pathophysiology of CSU involves degranulation of cutaneous mast cells and basophils leading to release of histamine and other mediators (e.g., cysteinyl leukotrienes, prostaglandins, platelet-activating factor, proinflammatory cytokines) that cause vasodilation, increased vascular permeability and pruritus, resulting in the characteristic wheals and angioedema (Lang 2022). Urticarial skin lesions have a predominantly lymphocytic infiltrate with occasional presence of eosinophils and neutrophils. Although mast cell activation is central to the underlying disease process of CSU, the precise mechanisms, especially in the absence of external triggers, remain incompletely understood.
L78: Standard of care consists of nonsedating H1-antihistamines as first-line therapy, with dose escalation up to the four times approved doses. Although most patients will experience a partial or complete response with antihistamines alone, approximately 40 to 65% will remain sufficiently symptomatic to require escalation in therapy. Current treatment guidelines recommend omalizumab as an approved second-line therapy; however, about 30% of patients have symptoms refractory to omalizumab. Dupilumab was recently approved for patients who remain symptomatic despite H1-antihistamines; however, the clinical program failed to demonstrate a benefit in patients who are refractory to omalizumab. Cyclosporin, used off-label, remains a third-line option in nonresponders, but requires close monitoring for adverse reactions. Though there are safe and effective therapies for CSU, a sizeable subset of refractory patients exist for whom additional therapeutic options would be beneficial.
L79: Remibrutinib (LOU064) is an oral BTK inhibitor that inhibits mast cell and basophil degranulation mediated by pathogenic immunoglobulin E (IgE) or immunoglobulin G (IgG) directed against the Fc epsilon receptor-1 (FcεRI) or IgE. It blocks IgE- and IgG-mediated FcεRI activation of mast cells and basophils and the subsequent release of histamine and other proinflammatory mediators that cause itch, hives, and angioedema.
L80: The proposed indication is for the treatment of CSU in adult patients who remain symptomatic despite H1-antihistamine treatment at a proposed dosage of 25 mg orally twice daily. BTK inhibition represents a novel therapeutic target for CSU.
L81: Although remibrutinib does not have breakthrough therapy designation for this indication nor meet criteria for a priority review, the Applicant submitted the NDA using the Rare Pediatric Disease Priority Review Voucher (NDA 217759); therefore, this application was granted a priority review. The following key efficacy and safety issues were identified during the review.

L92: ## 3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
L93: The efficacy and safety review of remibrutinib for the proposed indication of CSU in adult patients who remain symptomatic despite H1-antihistamines treatment is based on the results from two identical, pivotal, phase 3 trials, REMIX-1 (CLOU064A2301; referred to as Trial A2301) and REMIX-2 (CLOU064A2302; referred to as Trial A2302). The study protocols are described in Section 6.2.2. A detailed clinical pharmacology review is available in Section 5.2.
L94: Data from Trials A2301 and A2302 provided primary evidence evaluating the efficacy of remibrutinib versus placebo in the treatment of CSU. These data are presented in Section 6.2.2.8. For the evaluation of safety, data from Trials A2301 and A2302 were analyzed by the clinical data scientist using the R programming environment and the clinical reviewer using JMP
L95: Clinical and the Analysis Toolbox (Office of Computational Science). The safety results are presented in Section 7.

L119: ## 5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, and Clinical Pharmacology
L120: ## 5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential Effectiveness
L121: Remibrutinib is an oral, small molecule inhibitor of BTK, a member of the tyrosine kinase expressed (in hepatocellular carcinoma) (TEC) family of nonreceptor tyrosine kinases consisting of BTK, TEC, bone marrow X-linked kinase (BMX), interleukin-2 inducible T-cell kinase (ITK), and tyrosine kinase (TXK). It inhibits both unphosphorylated and phosphorylated BTK with nanomolar potency. BTK is expressed on mast cells, basophils, B-cells, macrophages, microglia, and platelets. In B-cells, BTK is an important mediator of B-cell receptor signaling involved with
L122: B-cell survival, proliferation, migration, and differentiation of memory B-cells in antibody producing plasma cells. In mast cells and basophils, BTK is a component of Fc\epsilonRI and Fc gamma receptor (Fc\gammaR) signaling. In platelets, BTK is implicated in aggregation and clot retraction.
L123: CSU is a chronic skin disease characterized by the presence of chronic (lasting \ge6 weeks with three to four occurrences/week) hives with no identifiable cause. Patients with CSU exhibit increased numbers of dermal mast cells and circulating inflammatory markers, including interleukin 6 (IL-6). In approximately 50% of patients with CSU, IgG autoantibodies against IgE or Fc\epsilonRI have been detected. In these cases, autoantibodies may activate mast cells and basophils resulting in the release of mediators such as histamine. Remibrutinib is proposed as an inhibitor of mast cell and basophil degranulation mediated by pathogenic IgE or IgG directed against the Fc\epsilonRI or IgE and blocker of IgE- and IgG-mediated Fc\epsilonRI activation of mast cells and basophils. In patients with CSU, remibrutinib prevents the release of histamine and other proinflammatory mediators that cause itch, hives, or angioedema. In vivo, remibrutinib showed a response in two animal models relevant to CSU, a mouse reverse passive Arthus model and a mouse passive cutaneous anaphylaxis model. These are models of skin mast cell degranulation induced by Fc\gammaR- and Fc epsilon receptor (Fc\epsilonR)-mediated hypersensitivity.
L124: ## Established Pharmacologic Class Discussion
L125: (b) (4) Remibrutinib inhibits human BTK with nanomolar potency (half-maximal inhibitory concentration [IC50]: 0.6 to 7.5nM) and is more selective for BTK compared to the BTK-related enzymes TEC and BMX (IC50: 124 to 141nM). However, clinical pharmacokinetic data from the proposed clinical dose of 25 mg BID suggests a remibrutinib concentration of approximately 128nM, which is sufficient to potentially inhibit the BTK-related kinases TEC and BMX (refer to Table 5 below). Therefore, the totality of in vitro and in vivo pharmacology data submitted support the established pharmacologic class of remibrutinib as a kinase inhibitor.

Range of effective dose(s) or exposure	
In the pivotal phase 3 trials (A2301 and A2302), 25 mg BID was the only dosage evaluated and demonstrated superiority to placebo on the coprimary efficacy endpoints of change from baseline to Week 12 in the weekly hives severity score (HSS7) and the weekly itch severity score (ISS7).
In a phase 2 dose-ranging study (A2201), all remibrutinib dosages evaluated, ranging from 10 mg QD to 100 mg BID demonstrated improvement over placebo in the primary endpoint of change from baseline to Week 4 in the weekly urticaria activity score (UAS7). Notably, the treatment effect was comparable across all dosages remibrutinib with no apparent dose-response.

Maximally tolerated dose or exposure	
In healthy subjects, the highest dosage of remibrutinib evaluated was 600 mg QD for 12 days in Study X2101 (see Section 14.2.1.1).
In subjects with CSU, the highest dosage of remibrutinib evaluated was 100 mg BID for 12 weeks in Study A2201 and 52 weeks in Study A2201E1 (see Sections 14.2.7, 17.3, and 17.4).
Including the double-blind and open-label period in the pivotal trials, subjects were exposed to remibrutinib 25 mg BID up to a maximum of 61 weeks (see Section 7.5).

Dose proportionality	
In Study A2201, remibrutinib steady-state Cmax and AUCtau increases in an approximately dose-proportional manner over the dosage range of 10 mg twice daily to 100 mg twice daily (0.4 to 4 times the highest approved recommended dosage) in subjects with CSU.
L135: ## NDA 218436
## RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
L136: Characteristic	Drug Information
Accumulation	Following the administration of multiple doses of 25 mg twice daily, Cmax increases 1.6-fold and AUC0-4 increases 2.7-fold. The AUC0-4 at steady state was obtained from Study A2201 following the administration of 25 mg BID, while the initial AUC0-4 was calculated from Study X2101 following administration of the first dose of 25 mg. Both studies, X2101 and A2201, employed an identical hard gelatin capsule formulation and bioanalytical method. The median body weight of subjects in both studies was 77 kg. Study X2101 was conducted in a generally healthy population, whereas Study A2201 was performed on subjects with CSU. Note that there is accumulation despite the short half-life of remibrutinib. This is likely due to the distribution/disposition of remibrutinib in human body.
Time to achieve steady state	Following twice daily dosing of remibrutinib, steady state was achieved by Day 2.
Bridge between to-be-marketed and clinical trial/study formulations	The to-be-marketed formulation (film-coated tablet) was used in all phase 3 trials. The interim formulation used during development was compared to the to-be-marketed formulation in a comparative BA study (X2105). Study X2105 was conducted to compare remibrutinib systemic exposure between the interim hard gelatin capsule formulation used in phase 1 and 2 clinical trials and the to-be-marketed film-coated tablet formulation. Relative to the hard gelatin capsule formulation, the film-coated tablet formulation yielded 28% lower Cmax and 20% lower AUC0-inf. Refer to Section 14.2.3 for details. These differences are considered small with no clinical relevance.
Bioavailability	The absolute bioavailability for the to-be-marketed film-coated tablet formulation was not determined.
Tmax	Median Tmax is approximately 1 hour under fasted conditions.
Food effect (fed/fasted)
Geometric least-squares mean and 90% CI	No clinically meaningful food effect was observed.
Following the administration of remibrutinib 25 mg BID using the to-be-marketed film-coated tablet formulation to healthy subjects, a standard high-fat meal delayed Tmax by approximately 1 hour compared to the Tmax in the fasted state. In comparison to the fasted state, the Cmax was slightly lower in the fed state with a geometric LSM ratio (90% CI) (fed/fasted) of 0.951 (0.763 to 1.19) and the AUC0-12h was slightly higher in the fed state with a geometric LSM ratio (90% CI) (fed/fasted) of 1.33 (1.19 to 1.49) (see Section 14.2.4).
Volume of distribution	Based on population PK analysis, remibrutinib volume of distribution at steady state was 58 L (central compartment) and 1180 L (peripheral compartment).
Plasma protein binding	Remibrutinib plasma protein binding is 95.4%
Drug as substrate of transporters	Remibrutinib is a substrate for P-glycoprotein (P-gp), but it is not a substrate for other tested efflux or uptake transporters.
L137: Characteristic	Drug Information
Mass balance results	
Elimination
The recovery of radioactivity following oral administration was incomplete, with only 56.2% of the administered dose being accounted for at the end of Day 8. The reason for this is unclear but the Applicant attributes this to technical issues. The majority of the recovered radioactivity was excreted in feces (31.8%) and then urine (24.4%). The recovery of intravenously administered [^{14}\text{C}]remibrutinib was nearly complete at the end of Day 4, with the majority of the radioactivity also recovered in feces (67% to 71.7%). About 26% to 30% of the radioactivity was recovered in the urine following IV dosing.
Following oral administration, remibrutinib was the predominant species in blood, constituting 16.7% of the blood ^{14}\text{C}-AUC_{0-24\text{h}}. The unchanged remibrutinib in excreta represented 1.86% of the administered dose and none of the metabolites accounted for \ge 10\% of the total. The amount of unchanged remibrutinib excreted in urine was minimal, constituting 0.65% of the oral dose, suggesting negligible renal excretion of remibrutinib. Following IV dosing, the amount of unchanged remibrutinib excreted in urine was 2.93%. In urine, none of the metabolites accounted for >10\% for the total. Remibrutinib is assumed to be completely absorbed (F_a > 98\%) and extensively metabolized, as only 1.21% of the oral dose was excreted in feces as unchanged remibrutinib. Unchanged remibrutinib was not detected in feces following intravenous administration. In feces, none of the metabolites accounted for >10\% for the total.
There was no specific distribution of drug-related components into blood cells following either intravenous or oral administration of remibrutinib. The AUC_{0-\text{inf}} of total radioactivity in plasma was marginally higher than that in whole blood (14900 h*ng-eq/mL versus 12700 h*ng-eq/mL) following oral administration. The AUC_{0-\text{inf}} of total radioactivity following intravenous administration in plasma and whole blood were also comparable (16.9 versus 18.5 h*ng-eq/mL in IV Arm A and 13.2 versus 12.8 h*ng-eq/mL in IV Arm B), indicating an equal distribution of total radioactivity between plasma and red blood cells. For additional details see Section 14.2.2.

Clearance	Based on population PK analysis, the apparent oral clearance of remibrutinib is 160 L/hr.
Half-life	The elimination half-life of remibrutinib is 1 to 2 hours.
Metabolic pathway(s)	Remibrutinib is primarily metabolized by CYP3A4.
Primary excretion pathways (% dose)	Following the oral administration of 100-mg radiolabeled remibrutinib, unchanged remibrutinib in excreta represented 1.86% of the administered dose (0.65% in urine and 1.21% in feces). Following IV administration of radiolabeled remibrutinib, the amount of unchanged remibrutinib in urine was 2.93% of the dose with none detected in feces. No metabolite in urine or feces accounted for at least 10% of total exposure following oral or IV dosing.

## RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
L139: Characteristic	Drug Information
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight	No clinically meaningful effect of body weight on the pharmacokinetics of remibrutinib was observed.
The impact of body weight on the pharmacokinetics of remibrutinib was assessed through the combined effects of fat-free mass (FFM) and fat mass (FM) using population PK analysis. Overall, no clinically significant differences in the pharmacokinetics of remibrutinib were observed over the body weight range of 39 to 162 kg.
Age	Population PK analyses indicated that age (18 to 80 years) does not significantly impact the pharmacokinetics of remibrutinib in subjects with CSU.
Race/Ethnicity	Based on population PK analysis, no clinically meaningful effect of race/ethnicity (59.3% non-Asian, 8.8% Mainland Chinese, 12.2% Japanese, and 19.7% other Asian) on the pharmacokinetics of remibrutinib was observed.
Renal impairment	No dosage adjustment is recommended in patients with mild, moderate, or severe renal impairment. Renal excretion of remibrutinib was found to account for <1% of total blood clearance. In the mass balance study, the amount of unchanged remibrutinib excreted in urine was ≤2.9% of the dose. In addition, population PK analysis indicated that creatinine clearance, ranging from 23 to 275 mL/min, did not significantly influence the clearance of remibrutinib.
Hepatic impairment	The use of remibrutinib should be avoided in patients with mild, moderate, or severe hepatic impairment (HI) (Child-Pugh Class A, B, or C).
Following the administration of remibrutinib at a dose of 25 mg twice daily, the geometric mean ratios for patients with mild, moderate, and severe HI compared to subjects with normal hepatic function were 1.85, 1.65, and 1.99, respectively, for Cmax, and 2.33, 2.3, and 3.49, respectively, for AUClast,ss (see Section 14.2.5).
Drug-Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator)
Inhibition/induction of metabolism or transporters system by remibrutinib	Coadministration of remibrutinib at 100 mg with a single dose of 0.25 mg digoxin (a P-gp substrate) increased digoxin Cmax by 2.1-fold and AUC by 1.4-fold (see Section 14.2.6.2).
Coadministration of remibrutinib at 100 mg with a single dose of 10-mg rosuvastatin (a BCRP and OATP1B substrate) increased rosuvastatin Cmax by 1.6-fold and AUC by 1.7-fold (see Section 14.2.6.2).
Remibrutinib at the recommended dose of 25 mg twice daily is not expected to have a clinically relevant effect on the pharmacokinetics of midazolam (CYP3A4 substrate), combined oral contraceptives containing ethinylestradiol and levonorgestrel (CYP3A4 substrate), tolbutamide (CYP2C9 substrate), caffeine (CYP1A2 substrate), and coproporphyrin I (endogenous OATP1B substrate) based on in vivo DDI studies conducted with high doses of remibrutinib (100 mg) and physiologically based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) simulations. Refer to Sections 14.2.6 and 14.5.3 for details.
L140: Characteristic	Drug Information
Effect of other drugs on remibrutinib	
Drug-Interaction Liability (Drug as Victim)
Remibrutinib C_{max} increased by 3.3-fold and AUC increased by 4.3-fold following concomitant administration with ritonavir 100 mg BID for 4 days (strong CYP3A4 inhibitor) (see Section 14.2.6.1).
Remibrutinib C_{max} increased by 1.24-fold and AUC increased by 1.3-fold when coadministered with grapefruit juice (see Section 14.2.6.1).
Remibrutinib C_{max} is predicted to increase by approximately 1.9-fold and AUC is predicted to increase by approximately 2.3-fold following concomitant administration with erythromycin (a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor) 500 mg four times a day for 7 days (see Section 14.5.3).
Remibrutinib C_{max} decreased by 74% and AUC decreased by 77% following concomitant administration with carbamazepine (a strong CYP3A4 inducer) 300 mg twice daily for 14 days (see Section 14.2.6.2).
Remibrutinib C_{max} is predicted to decrease by approximately 60% and AUC is predicted to decrease by approximately 64% following concomitant administration with efavirenz (a moderate CYP3A4 inducer) 600 mg once daily for 14 days (see Section 14.5.3).
No clinically relevant changes in remibrutinib exposure were observed among subjects with CSU who were concomitantly taking acid-reducing agents.
The risks and benefits of coadministration of antithrombotic agents with remibrutinib should be considered due to the potential platelet inhibitory effect of remibrutinib as a BTK inhibitor. No PK data are available for the coadministered anticoagulants in phase 3 trials. Exposure-response analysis indicated no significant relationship between the incidence of bleeding and increasing remibrutinib concentration at steady state.

L143: ## 6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
L144: ## 6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential Effectiveness
L145: ## Supporting Evidence for the Selected Dose
L146: ## Dose-Ranging
L147: The selection of remibrutinib 25 mg BID dosing for the pivotal phase 3 clinical Trials A2301 and A2302 was based on findings from a dose-ranging phase 2 study (A2201) (see Section 14.2.7). In Study A2201, adult subjects with CSU who remained inadequately controlled on second-generation H1-antihistamines were randomized to receive remibrutinib at various dosing regimens: 10 mg once daily (QD), 35 mg QD, 100 mg QD, 10 mg BID, 25 mg BID, 100 mg BID, or placebo. The primary endpoint in Study A2201 was the change from baseline in the UAS7 at Week 4 compared to placebo. The estimated difference in least-squares means relative to placebo were as follows: -13.66 for 10 mg QD, -13.64 for 35 mg QD, -9.21 for 100 mg QD, -10.55 for 10 mg BID, -14.58 for 25 mg BID, and -12.62 for 100 mg BID. Notably, all dose groups demonstrated an improvement over placebo in UAS7 at Week 4. The treatment effect was comparable across all dose groups evaluated in Study A2201 but was numerically highest among subjects randomized to receive the 25-mg BID dosage. This finding is further corroborated by the dose-response relationship, which demonstrated that all dosages lie on the plateau region of the response curve (Figure 1). Importantly, the observed dose-response relationship is defined based on improvement over the placebo as there is no apparent difference between any of the administered dosing regimens, irrespective of dosing frequency.
L148: **Figure 1. Dose-Response Relationship Based on Change From Baseline UAS7 Score at Week 4 in the Dose-Ranging Study A2201**
L149: Source: Study A2201 Clinical Report, Figure 11-1
L150: The dose is displayed as total daily dose.
L151: The dose-response curve shows the median value from 5000 bootstrap samples.
L152: The shaded area displays the 90% prediction interval from 5000 bootstrap samples.
L153: The dots with the error bar represent LS mean and 90% CI from the MMRM.
L154: Abbreviations: BID, twice daily; CI, confidence interval; LS mean, least-squares mean; MMRM, mixed model for repeated measures;
L155: QD, once daily; UAS7, weekly urticaria activity score
L156: Similar results were observed for numerous secondary endpoints, including the change from baseline to Week 12 in UAS7, the change from baseline in UAS7 over time, the proportion of subjects achieving a UAS7 score of 0 at Week 12, and the proportion of subjects achieving a UAS7 score \le 6 at Week 12. For all endpoints, improvement over placebo was demonstrated at all dosages of remibrutinib, with numerically greatest response observed with the 25-mg BID dosage (refer to Figure 11-2, and Tables 11-4 and 11-5, clinical study report for Study A2201).
L157: Based on the relatively flat dose-response observed in Study A2201, the Agency recommended evaluating more than one remibrutinib dose in phase 3 trials; however, the Applicant chose to carry forward a single nominal dose and dosing regimen of remibrutinib 25 mg BID. Although 25-mg BID remibrutinib resulted in the numerically greatest response for the primary endpoint and numerous secondary endpoints, Study A2201 was not designed to evaluate for significant differences between the various remibrutinib dosing regimens. All dosages demonstrated improvement over placebo with overlap observed in the treatment effects.
L158: ## Phase 3 Trials
L159: In pivotal phase 3 Trials A2301 and A2302, 25 mg BID was the only dosing regimen evaluated in adult subjects with CSU inadequately controlled by H1-antihistamines. For full details on the study designs and efficacy results from Trials A2301 and A2302, refer to Sections 6.2.1 and 6.2.2, respectively. In both trials, treatment with 25-mg BID remibrutinib yielded significant improvements over placebo in the coprimary efficacy endpoints of change from baseline to Week 12 in the HSS7 and the ISS7.
L160: ## Exposure-Response Analyses
L161: The 25-mg BID dosing regimen was also supported by exposure-response (E-R) analyses, which evaluated the correlation between the average steady-state concentration of remibrutinib and changes from baseline in ISS7, HSS7, and UAS7. Efficacy data and simulated average steady-state concentrations of remibrutinib at six dose levels (10 mg QD, 35 mg QD, 100 mg QD, 10 mg BID, 25 mg BID, 100 mg BID) plus placebo from the dose-ranging phase 2 study (A2201) and the two phase 3 trials (A2301 and A2302) were included in the E-R analyses. Analyses demonstrated a maximum induction effect (E_{max}) type of E-R relationship across all measured endpoints, with the plateau of therapeutic effect achieved at relatively low drug exposures (Figure 2). Refer to Section 14.5.2 for details. Results of the E-R analyses are similar to the dose-response relationship observed in Study A2201. The E-R relationship appears to be primarily driven by the difference from the placebo group as there was little difference in efficacy across quartiles of average concentrations at steady state.

L167: The E-R analyses for safety, based on data from the phase 2 study (A2201) and the two phase 3 trials (A2301 and A2302), did not identify a significant relationship between remibrutinib exposure and adverse events (AEs), serious adverse events (SAEs), or AEs of special interest (bleeding, cytopenia, and infection). Refer to Section 14.5.2 for details.
L168: The available data indicate that the proposed dosage of 25 mg BID is appropriate for the treatment of adult patients with CSU who remain symptomatic despite H1-antihistamine treatment.
L169: ## 6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To Demonstrate Efficacy
L170: ## 6.2.1. Efficacy Trials, A2301 and A2302
L171: The efficacy of remibrutinib for CSU was primarily demonstrated through two large, identically designed phase 3 pivotal trials: CLOU064A2301 (A2301) and CLOU064A2302 (A2302). These were global, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trials that evaluated remibrutinib 25 mg twice daily versus placebo in adult subjects with CSU inadequately controlled by H1-antihistamines. Both trials had a 52-week treatment period, consisting of a 24-week placebo-
L172: controlled treatment period followed by a 28-week single-arm, unblinded treatment period. The primary efficacy analysis was conducted at Week 12.
L173: ## 6.2.2. Trials A2301 and A2302
L174: ## 6.2.2.1. Design and Conduct, Trials A2301 and A2302
L175: Given that Trials A2301 (REMIX-1) and A2302 (REMIX-2) were identical in design, the protocols are discussed in tandem. Both were multicenter, 52-week, randomized, double-blind, parallel-group, placebo-controlled phase 3 trials investigating the efficacy, safety, and tolerability of remibrutinib 25 mg twice daily in adult subjects with CSU inadequately controlled by H1-antihistamines. After obtaining informed consent, subjects entered a screening period of up to 4 weeks during which subjects underwent an evaluation to determine their eligibility. The screening visit included a physical exam, collection of demographic information, height and weight measurements, vital signs assessment, and electrocardiogram (ECG) recording. Subjects received their electronic (device) diary (eDiary) during the screening period, which was used throughout the study to complete the urticaria patient daily diary (UPDD) containing the urticaria activity score (UAS) and angioedema activity score (AAS).
L176: Eligible subjects were randomized in a 2:1 ratio to receive either remibrutinib 25 mg or matching placebo orally twice daily for 24 weeks. Following the double-blind, placebo-controlled treatment period, all subjects received open-label remibrutinib for the remaining 28 weeks of the trial. After completing the 52-week treatment period, subjects were followed for 4 weeks off-treatment. The study schematic is shown in Figure 3. The schedule of study assessments is in Section 15.1.1.
L177: Figure 3. Study Design, Trials A2301 and A2302
L178: D-1: Day 1, b.i.d.: bis in die/twice a day, mg: milligram(s), Pbo: placebo, PA: Primary Analysis, PE: Primary Endpoint, Scr: Screening, W: Week
L179: Source: Applicant-provided CSRs, p. 40
L180: ## 6.2.2.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trials A2301 and A2302
L181: Key eligibility criteria for the trials are summarized below.
L182: ## Inclusion Criteria
L183: - Male and female adult subjects \ge 18 years of age at the time of screening.
- CSU duration for \ge 6 months prior to screening (defined as the onset of CSU determined by the investigator based on all available supporting documentation).
- Diagnosis of CSU inadequately controlled by second-generation H1-antihistamines at the time of randomization defined as:Presence of itch and hives for \ge 6 consecutive weeks prior to screening despite use of second-generation H1-antihistamines during this time period
- UAS7 score (range 0 to 42) \ge 16, ISS7 score (range 0 to 21) \ge 6 and HSS7 score (range 0 to 21) \ge 6 during the 7 days prior to randomization (Day 1)
- Documentation of hives within 3 months before randomization (either at screening and/or at randomization; or documented in the subjects' medical history).
- Subjects must not have had more than one missing UPDD entry (either morning or evening) in the 7 days prior to randomization during screening (Day 1).
L184: ## Exclusion Criteria
L185: - Clearly defined predominant or sole trigger of chronic urticaria (chronic inducible urticaria) including urticaria factitia (symptomatic dermographism), cold, heat, solar, pressure, delayed pressure, aquagenic, cholinergic, or contact urticaria.
- Other diseases with symptoms of urticaria or angioedema, including but not limited to urticaria vasculitis, urticaria pigmentosa, erythema multiforme, mastocytosis, hereditary urticaria, or drug-induced urticaria.
- Any other skin disease associated with chronic itching that might influence the investigator's opinion, the trial evaluations, or results, e.g., atopic dermatitis, bullous pemphigoid, dermatitis herpetiformis, senile pruritus or psoriasis.
- Use of prohibited medications (see Table 99).
- Known history or evidence of ongoing alcohol or drug abuse within the last 6 months before randomization.
- History of malignancy of any organ system (other than localized basal cell carcinoma of the skin or in situ cervical cancer), treated or untreated, within the past 5 years, regardless of whether there was evidence of local recurrence or metastases.
- Pregnancy or lactation.
L186: - Women of child-bearing potential,1 defined as all women physiologically capable of becoming pregnant, unless using highly effective methods of contraception during dosing and for 7 days after stopping of study treatment. Highly effective contraception methods included:Total abstinence (when in line with preferred and usual lifestyle of the subject). Periodic abstinence (e.g., calendar, ovulation, symptothermal, postovulation methods) and withdrawal were not acceptable methods of contraception.
- Female sterilization (surgical bilateral oophorectomy with or without hysterectomy), total hysterectomy, or bilateral tubal ligation at least 6 weeks before taking investigational drug. Oophorectomy alone must have been confirmed by follow-up hormone level assessment.
- Male sterilization (at least 6 months prior to screening). For female subjects in the trial, the vasectomized male partner should be the sole partner for that subject.
- Use of oral (estrogen and progesterone), injected, or implanted hormonal methods of contraception or other forms of hormonal contraception that have comparable efficacy (failure rate &lt;1%), for example hormone vaginal ring, or transdermal hormone contraception, or placement of an intrauterine device or intrauterine system. In case of use of oral contraception, women should have been stable on the same pill for a minimum of 3 months before taking the investigational drug. If local regulations deviate from the contraception methods listed above to prevent pregnancy, local regulations apply and will be described in the informed consent form.
- Major surgery within 8 weeks prior to screening or planned surgery for the duration of the trial.
- History of live attenuated vaccine within 6 weeks prior to randomization or requirement to receive these vaccinations at any time during study drug treatment.
- Evidence of clinically significant cardiovascular (such as, but not limited to myocardial infarction, unstable ischemic heart disease, New York Heart Association Functional Classification Class III/IV left ventricular failure, arrhythmia and uncontrolled hypertension within 12 months prior to Visit 1), neurological, psychiatric, pulmonary, renal, hepatic, endocrine, metabolic, hematological disorders, gastrointestinal disease or immunodeficiency that, in the investigator's opinion, would compromise the safety of the subject, interfere with the interpretation of the trial results or otherwise preclude participation or protocol adherence of the subject.
- Uncontrolled disease states, such as asthma, or inflammatory bowel disease, where flares are commonly treated with oral or parenteral corticosteroids.
L187: 1 Women were considered not of child-bearing potential if they were postmenopausal or had surgical bilateral oophorectomy (with or without hysterectomy), total hysterectomy or bilateral tubal ligation at least six weeks prior to screening. In the case of oophorectomy alone, only when the reproductive status of the woman had been confirmed by follow-up hormone level assessment was she considered not of child-bearing potential. Women were considered postmenopausal if they had 12 months of natural (spontaneous) amenorrhea with an appropriate clinical profile (e.g. age appropriate, history of vasomotor symptoms).
L188: - Hematology parameters at screening:Hemoglobin: &lt;10 g/dL
- Platelets: &lt;100000/mm3
- Leucocytes: &lt;3000/mm3
- Neutrophils: &lt;1500/mm3
- Significant bleeding risk or coagulation disorders.
- History of gastrointestinal bleeding, e.g., in association with use of nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs, that was clinically relevant (e.g., requiring hospitalization or blood transfusion).
- Requirement for antiplatelet medication, except for acetylsalicylic acid up to 100 mg/day or clopidogrel. The use of dual antiplatelet therapy (e.g., acetylsalicylic acid + clopidogrel) was prohibited.
- Requirement for anticoagulant medication (for example, warfarin or novel oral anticoagulants).
- History or current hepatic disease including but not limited to acute or chronic hepatitis, cirrhosis or hepatic failure or aspartate aminotransferase (AST)/alanine aminotransferase (ALT) levels of more than 1.5x upper limit of normal (ULN) or international normalized ratio (INR) of more than 1.5 at screening.
- History of renal disease, creatinine level above 1.5x ULN, or estimated glomerular filtration rate &lt;45 mL/min (using the Cockcroft-Gault equation) at screening.
- Evidence of an ongoing hepatitis C infection (e.g., defined by the detection of hepatitis C RNA at screening) and/or an ongoing hepatitis B infection (defined by the detection of hepatitis B virus surface antigen [HBsAg] and/or hepatitis B virus [HBV]-DNA at screening; subjects who are positive for antihepatitis B core antibodies but who are negative for antibodies against HBsAg and HBV-DNA can be included into the trial if they agree to monitoring for HBsAg and HBV-DNA reactivation).
- Known or suspected ongoing, chronic, or recurrent infectious disease including but not limited to opportunistic infections (e.g., tuberculosis, atypical mycobacterioses, listeriosis or aspergillosis) and/or known positivity for HIV infection. HIV antigen/antibody tests will be performed to determine HIV status if required according to local regulations.
- Use of other investigational drugs within five half-lives or within 30 days (for small molecules) prior to screening or until the expected pharmacodynamic (PD) effect has returned to baseline (for biologics), whichever is longer; or longer if required by local regulations.
- Previous use of remibrutinib or other BTK inhibitors.
- History of hypersensitivity to any of the study treatments or its excipients or to drugs of similar chemical classes.
L189: ## 6.2.2.3. Study Treatments, Trials A2301 and A2302
L190: Remibrutinib was formulated as 25-mg film-coated tablets (FCT). There was a formulation change between phase 2 and 3 trials from a hard gelatin capsule to FCT; for additional information on formulations evaluated in previous studies, see Section 14.2. The investigational medicinal product (IMP) was packaged and labeled according to the International Council for Harmonisation (ICH) good manufacturing process and good clinical practice guidelines. Subjects were treated with one of the following IMPs according to the assigned treatment sequence:
L191: - Remibrutinib 25-mg FCT administered orally twice daily
- Matching placebo FCT administered orally twice daily
L192: Each subject was instructed to take one FCT in the morning and one FCT in the evening. Compliance was assessed by the investigator and/or study personnel in the drug accountability form at each study visit.
L193: ## Permitted Interruptions of Study Treatment
L194: Study treatment interruptions were permitted under predefined circumstances to manage specific safety events. For bleeding event management, treatment interruption was required 7 days before planned surgery with clinically significant bleeding risk. Following surgical recovery, subjects could resume study treatment after a 7-day period, ensuring adequate hemostatic recovery while minimizing treatment exposure gaps. In cases of hepatitis B reactivation, defined as new appearance of detectable HBV-DNA or positive HBsAg, subjects were required to initiate antiviral treatment according to local clinical practice standards and interrupt study treatment. Study treatment resumption was permitted only after HBV-DNA and HBsAg reached undetectable levels, ensuring viral suppression before BTK inhibitor re-exposure.
L195: ## Discontinuations
L196: Treatment discontinuation occurred when study medication was permanently stopped before the protocol-specified completion date and was considered an intercurrent event handled with treatment policy strategy.
L197: ## 6.2.2.4. Concomitant Medications, Trials A2301 and A2302
L198: ## Concomitant Treatment
L199: All subjects were required to maintain second-generation H1-antihistamine therapy at locally approved doses as mandatory background therapy throughout the trial duration. Background H1-antihistamine therapy was required to remain unchanged until the Week 12 visit to ensure consistent baseline treatment during the primary efficacy evaluation period. Modifications to background therapy were permitted after Week 12 only when medically required, specifically in cases where adverse reactions were attributable to the background therapy as determined by the investigator's clinical judgment.
L200: ## Rescue Medications
L201: Second-generation H1-antihistamines, different from those used as background medication, were permitted as rescue therapy on an as-needed basis for subjects experiencing CSU flare-ups with
L202: unbearable symptoms during screening, treatment, and follow-up periods. The daily dose of these rescue H1-antihistamines was limited to no more than four times the locally approved dose, following current urticaria treatment guidelines.
L203: Oral corticosteroid use was prohibited prior to Week 12 to prevent interference with the primary endpoint assessment. After Week 12, subjects could use oral corticosteroids as rescue medication for unbearable CSU flare-ups, but usage was limited to a maximum of 3 days within any 30-day period and no more than 9 total days after Week 12. The recommended corticosteroid dose was 20 to 50 mg prednisone or equivalent per day, consistent with current urticaria treatment guidelines.
L204: Intake of rescue medication as per protocol prior to Week 12 was considered an intercurrent event handled via treatment policy strategy for data analysis.
L205: ## **Prohibited Medication**
L206: Biologic therapies were not permitted due to their potential to independently improve chronic spontaneous urticaria symptoms and confound efficacy evaluations. Routine oral corticosteroids, defined as more than three doses over a 5-day period, were prohibited, although oral corticosteroids were permitted as additional rescue therapy for chronic spontaneous urticaria after Week 12. For a full list of the medications prohibited during Trials A2301 and A2302 refer to Table 99 in Section 15.1.2.
L207: Composite strategy was applied in the setting of intake of strongly confounding prohibited medication including:
L208: - Biologics treatment at any time before Week 12
- Cyclosporin after Week 8 to Week 12
- Systemic corticosteroids after Week 8 to Week 12
L209: Measurements after this event were excluded from the analysis and were replaced with the worst value of the endpoint.
L210: ## **6.2.2.5. Efficacy Assessments, Trials A2301 and A2302**
L211: ## **Patient Reported Outcomes**
L212: All subjects were provided with an eDiary that contained the PROs that measured symptom severity and effect on daily activities. The PROs used for primary and secondary efficacy endpoints were captured in the eDiary that contained the UPDD and the AAS. Subjects completed eDiary assessments twice daily for the UPDD and once daily for the AAS (if prompted by presence of angioedema symptoms) from screening through Week 52 or until trial discontinuation. Other PRO questionnaires were administered during trial site visits (or in the eDiary at the scheduled time if unable to attend in person).
L213: The UPDD assessed severity of itch and number of hives, use of rescue medication, sleep and activity interference, angioedema occurrence and management, and healthcare utilization (see
L214: NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
L215: Figure 65 for a copy of the full instrument). Relevant components of the instrument used for efficacy endpoints included:
L216: - Itch severity score (ISS): Itch intensity was reported twice daily on a scale from 0 to 3 (0 = none, 1 = mild, 2 = moderate 3 = severe). The ISS7 was calculated by summing the average daily ISS scores over 7 consecutive days (range 0 to 21).
- Hives severity score (HSS): Hives severity was reported twice daily on a scale from 0 to 3 (0 = none, 1 = 1 to 6 hives, 2 = 7 to 12 hives, 3 = greater than 12 hives) (see Figure 65). The HSS7 was calculated by summing the average daily HSS scores over 7 consecutive days (range 0 to 21). The scoring system for hives has differed across CSU development programs as some HSS instruments score hives as 1 = &lt;20 wheals, 2 = 20 to 50 wheals, 3 = &gt;50 wheals. The HSS in this trial utilizes a more conservative scoring system.
- UAS: The UAS is a composite of the ISS and HSS. The UAS7 is the sum of daily ISS and HSS scores over 7 consecutive days (range 0 to 42). The UAS7 is an established and accepted PRO measure used to assess disease activity in chronic urticaria and considered fit-for-purpose for the proposed context of use in CSU (Hawro et al. 2018; Zuberbier et al. 2018).
L217: The AAS, as shown in Figure 66, was completed daily and consists of five items, each scored 0 to 3 points. The daily score ranges from 0 to 15, with higher scores indicating greater disease activity. The assessment began with an opening question from UPDD regarding the occurrence of angioedema. When subjects responded “no” to this initial question, the AAS score for that day was assigned a value of 0. Conversely, when subjects responded “yes,” they proceeded to complete additional questions that evaluated the duration, severity, and impact of the angioedema on both daily functioning and appearance. Each response field within the assessment received a score ranging from 0 to 3 points, with higher scores indicating greater severity. For this trial, AAS results were reported as a weekly angioedema activity score (AAS7), with possible scores ranging from a minimum of 0 to a maximum of 105. Although this tool has been described in the literature to assess disease activity in recurrent angioedema (Weller et al. 2013; Zuberbier et al. 2018; Kulthan et al. 2019), it has not yet been used to support regulatory decision making (b) (4).
L218: Other PROs captured in the trials included the dermatology life quality index (DLQI), EuroQol 5-dimension 5-level, work productivity and activity impairment, urticaria control test, patient global impression of severity, and patient global impression of change. As these PROs are not considered fit-for-purpose for the proposed context of use and/or were not proposed as multiplicity-controlled endpoints or labeling claims, they are not discussed further in this review.
L219: ## **Biomarkers**
L220: Additional assessments included biomarker evaluations measuring total immunoglobulin levels, peripheral blood B-cell counts and subsets, and the chronic urticaria index, an in vitro basophil histamine release assay in which a subject's serum is mixed with donor basophils and the released histamine levels are measured through a quantitative enzyme immunoassay. A chronic urticaria index value of greater than or equal to 10 presumably indicates that the subject has either an autoimmune basis for their disease (autoantibodies for IgE, Fc\epsilonRI, or anti-Fc\epsilonRII) or an alternate histamine-releasing factor.
L221: ## 6.2.2.6. Efficacy Endpoints, Trials A2301 and A2302
L222: ## Coprimary Efficacy Endpoints
L223: For the United States (based on FDA advice during development), the Applicant designated the following as coprimary efficacy endpoints:
L224: - Absolute change from baseline in ISS7 compared to placebo at Week 12
- Absolute change from baseline in HSS7 compared to placebo at Week 12
L225: ## Secondary Endpoints
L226: - Absolute change from baseline in UAS7 compared to placebo at Week 12.
- Proportion of subjects achieving UAS7 \le6 (yes/no) at Week 12.
- Proportion of subjects achieving UAS7 =0 (yes/no) at Week 12.
- Proportion of subjects achieving UAS7 \le6 (yes/no) at Week 2.
- Proportion of subjects achieving DLQI =0 to 1 (yes/no) at Week 12.
- Cumulative number of weeks that subjects achieve UAS7 \le6 responses between baseline and Week 12.
- Cumulative number of weeks that subjects achieve AAS7 =0 responses between baseline and Week 12.
L227: ## 6.2.2.7. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trials A2301 and A2302
L228: The statistical analysis plan for Trial A2301 is Amendment 4 dated January 25, 2024, and submitted to the FDA on February 5, 2024. The statistical analysis plan for Trial A2302 is Amendment 5 dated January 25, 2024, and was submitted to the FDA on February 5, 2024. Trial A2302 was identical to Trial A2301 in its design and analysis.
L229: ## Analysis Sets
L230: The three defined analysis sets were the full analysis set (FAS), safety analysis set (SAF), and randomized analysis set (RAS).
L231: ## FAS
L232: The FAS included all subjects to whom study treatment was assigned by randomization but excluded mis-randomized subjects. Mis-randomized subjects were defined as cases where interactive response technology contacts were made by the site either prematurely or inappropriately prior to confirmation of the subject's final randomization eligibility and no study medication was administered to the subject. The FAS was used for the coprimary efficacy endpoints and all other efficacy analyses, unless otherwise stated.
L233: ## SAF
L234: The SAF that included all subjects who received at least one dose of study treatment, regardless of randomization, and subjects were analyzed according to the study treatment received. The
L235: actual treatment was defined as the treatment received over the trial. In case of error in dispensation, the actual treatment corresponded to the treatment which was given most often. Safety set for the entire study period includes the subjects from remibrutinib 25 mg and placebo. The data from the open-label period for subjects who transitioned to remibrutinib 25 mg were not included in the safety analysis. Safety analyses were conducted using the SAF.
L236: ## RAS
L237: The RAS consisted of all randomized subjects, regardless of whether or not they received a dose of study drug, and subjects were analyzed according to the treatment they were assigned.
L238: ## Intercurrent Events
L239: For efficacy analyses, treatment policy strategy was used to handle the intercurrent events of discontinuation of study treatment due to any reason and the intake of rescue medication, switch of background medication, intake of other prohibited medication, or subjects noncompliant to treatment prior to Week 12. The Applicant's prespecified analysis used the composite strategy with values replaced by the worst value of the endpoint to handle the intercurrent event of strongly confounding prohibiting medication (e.g., biologics treatment at any time before Week 12, cyclosporin after Week 8, systemic corticosteroids after Week 8). However, the treatment policy strategy for all intercurrent events was recommended to the Applicant during the planning and is of primary regulatory interest. The results presented in this review correspond to the Applicant's prespecified intercurrent event handling strategy and comparisons of the analyses of the two estimands are discussed where relevant.
L240: ## Primary Analysis
L241: The primary analysis used a linear mixed model for repeated measures (MMRM) with data up to Week 12 to estimate treatment differences for change from baseline in ISS7 score (respectively HSS7) at Week 12. The MMRM included treatment group, baseline ISS7 score (respectively HSS7), randomization strata variables (region, prior exposure to anti-IgE biologics), week, the interaction of treatment by week, and the interaction of baseline ISS7 score (respectively HSS7) by week as fixed effects. Repeated measures within subject were modeled using an unstructured covariance of the error terms. The treatment difference at Week 12 was estimated using the least-squares (LS) mean and 95% confidence interval, along with the corresponding p-value for treatment comparison. The review team additionally considered a comparable analysis at Week 24, with details described in Section 15.1.4.2.
L242: In the case of missing data prior to Week 12, the missing data were to be handled following the missing-at-random (MAR) assumption. For the intercurrent event of strongly confounding prohibiting medication, the values for all visits after the intercurrent event were replaced with the worst value of the endpoint. Treatment policy strategy was planned for the intercurrent event of discontinuation of study treatment prior to Week 12 due to any reasons. The Applicant described data after the intercurrent event of treatment discontinuation as retrieved dropout (RDO) data. If no RDO data were collected after the intercurrent event, missing data were imputed based on the following rules. For subjects in the active treatment arm, if \ge 50\% of subjects had RDO data at Week 12, missing data were to be imputed using multiple imputation (MI) based on observed RDO data in the active arm. If &lt;50\% of subjects had RDO data at Week 12, missing data were to be imputed based on observed data in the placebo arm under the jump-to-reference assumption
L243: using MI. For subjects in the placebo arm, if \ge 50\% of subjects had RDO data at Week 12, missing data were to be imputed using MI based on observed RDO placebo arm data. If &lt; 50\% of subjects had RDO data at Week 12, missing data were to be imputed using MI under the MAR assumption based on the observed placebo arm data.
L244: Sensitivity, supplementary, and subgroups analyses were conducted to evaluate the robustness of the primary analysis and the impact of baseline disease characteristics on efficacy. For details, see Sections 15.1.4.3, 15.1.4.4, and 15.1.4.6.
L245: ## **Secondary Endpoints**
L246: A closed testing procedure was prespecified to test the coprimary and key secondary endpoints with control of family-wise type I error at the one-sided \alpha = 0.025. The testing is strictly hierarchical, so that null hypotheses can be tested along the predefined order at the level assigned until a null hypothesis cannot be rejected, at which point the testing stops. If the null hypotheses for the coprimary endpoints were rejected, then testing proceeded sequentially to test for the following:
L247: - Change from baseline in UAS7 score at Week 12
- Proportion of subjects with UAS7 \le 6 at Week 12
- Proportion of subjects with UAS7 = 0 at Week 12
- Proportion of subjects with UAS7 \le 6 at Week 2
- Proportion of subjects with DLQI = 0/1 at Week 12
- Cumulative number of weeks achieving UAS7 \le 6 response between baseline and Week 12
- Cumulative number of weeks achieving AAS7 = 0 response between baseline and Week 12
L248: The analysis methods for the key secondary endpoints are as follows:
L249: The treatment difference for change from baseline in UAS7 score at Week 12 was estimated via a linear MMRM with the same specification as for the coprimary endpoints.
L250: The number of subjects with UAS7 \le 6 at Week 12 was analyzed using a logistic regression model including treatment group, region, prior exposure to anti-IgE biologics, and baseline UAS7 score as covariates. The number of subjects with UAS7 = 0 at Week 12, UAS7 \le 6 at Week 2, and DLQI = 0/1 at Week 12 were analyzed similarly. However, DLQI is not considered to be a well-defined and reliable tool to estimate clinical benefit for labeling claims due to issues surrounding content validity and the interpretability of the complex, distal concepts captured in the instrument.
L251: The cumulative number of weeks achieving UAS7 \le 6 response between baseline and Week 12 was modelled using a negative binomial regression model with log link, and covariates of treatment group, region, and prior exposure to anti-IgE biologics and the subject's time in the treatment period up to Week 12 (natural log of proportion of time in treatment period, i.e., natural log of number of weeks with the response variable in treatment period/12 weeks) as an offset variable. The cumulative number of weeks achieving AAS7 = 0 response between baseline and Week 12 was analyzed similarly.
L252: ## 6.2.2.8. Results of Analyses, Trials A2301 and A2302
L253: This section summarizes the subject disposition, baseline demographics and clinical characteristics, and primary and key secondary efficacy results from Trial A2301 to support the efficacy of remibrutinib in subjects with CSU inadequately controlled by H1-antihistamines.
L254: ## 6.2.2.8.1. Subject Disposition, Trials A2301 and A2302
L255: A total of 925 subjects were enrolled across both pivotal phase 3 trials, with 912 subjects comprising the efficacy (FAS) and safety (SAF) analysis populations. Trial A2301 enrolled 470 subjects (313 randomized to remibrutinib 25 mg BID and 157 to placebo), while Trial A2302 enrolled 455 subjects (300 randomized to remibrutinib 25 mg BID and 155 to placebo) as shown in Table 8. Some subjects were enrolled and randomized in error (e.g., failed to meet eligibility criteria), and therefore included in the randomized analysis set but excluded from the FAS and SAF populations because no IMP was administered. The trials utilized identical randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled designs with a 2:1 randomization ratio favoring the active treatment arm.

L259: Subject disposition during the 24-week double-blind treatment periods is shown in Table 9, below. The completion rates across treatment groups were acceptable and relatively balanced without an excessive number of dropouts. The most commonly cited reason for treatment discontinuation was ‘subject decision’ which was comparable between arms. Notably, unsatisfactory therapeutic effect was more frequently cited as a discontinuation reason in the
L260: placebo group compared to the remibrutinib group, which is consistent with the efficacy of active treatment.

L265: ## 6.2.2.8.2. Baseline Demographics and Clinical Characteristics, Trials A2301 and A2302
L266: The baseline demographics of the FAS population in Trials A2301 and A2302 are shown in Table 10. The study populations were representative of the intended CSU patient population who are predominantly middle-aged females, and there were no major differences between treatment groups. The racial composition reflected the global nature of the trials, with subjects predominantly White or Asian, with comparable proportions across treatment groups. Approximately 20 to 25% of trial subjects were enrolled from U.S. sites. However, international treatment guidelines for CSU exist and standard of care is similar across countries. Therefore, results obtained from sites outside the United States should be applicable to a U.S. population.

L272: Baseline disease characteristics of the FAS populations in Trials A2301 and A2302 are shown in Table 11. The study populations reported a relatively high symptom burden at baseline as measured by baseline UAS7 scores, with no major difference between treatment groups. However, an imbalance was noted in angioedema burden, with subjects in the remibrutinib group having higher baseline angioedema activity as measured by mean AAS7 scores and fewer subjects being angioedema-free at baseline. Most subjects in the trials were naïve to prior anti-IgE therapy, indicating that the study populations did not represent subjects refractory to all available therapies.

L281: ## 6.2.2.8.3. Primary Efficacy Results, Trials A2301 and A2302
L282: Table 12 and Table 13 summarize the results for Trials A2301 and A2302 for the coprimary efficacy endpoints of change from baseline to Week 12 in HSS7 and ISS7, respectively. After adjusting for baseline HSS7 score, geographical region, prior exposure to anti-IgE biologics, visit week, treatment by visit interaction, and baseline HSS7 score by visit interaction, the estimated LS mean changes from baseline in HSS7 scores at Week 12 were greater in magnitude in the remibrutinib groups (LS means =-10.47 [SE 0.40] and -10.47 [SE 0.39]), compared to placebo (LS means =-6.86 [SE 0.55] and -6.00 [SE 0.53]) in Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively. For HSS7, the estimated differences in LS means relative to placebo were -3.61 (95% CI: -4.85, -2.36) and -4.47 (95% CI: -5.71, -3.23) in Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively. Similarly, for ISS7, after adjusting for similar covariates, the estimated least-squares mean changes from baseline in ISS7 scores at Week 12 were greater in magnitude in the remibrutinib groups (LS mean =-9.52 [SE 0.34] and -8.95 [SE 0.34]) compared to placebo (LS mean =-6.89 [SE 0.47] and -5.72 [SE 0.45]) in Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively. The estimated differences in LS means relative to placebo were -2.63 (95% CI: -3.70, -1.56) and -3.23 (95% CI: -4.29, -2.16), respectively. The coprimary endpoints in both trials were statistically significant with p-values &lt;0.001. Results of analyses which correspond to the estimand with treatment policy strategy for all intercurrent events were substantially similar, with p-values &lt;0.001 and small differences in point estimates and confidence intervals (at the most in the tenths place).

L289: In Trial A2301, one subject in the remibrutinib 25-mg group experienced the intercurrent event of intake of strongly confounding medication (see Section 6.2.2.4) prior to Week 12; results of the primary and key secondary analyses were minimally impacted by the handling of this intercurrent event with composite versus treatment policy strategy. In Trial A2302, one subject in the remibrutinib 25-mg group and one subject in the placebo group experienced the intercurrent event of intake of strongly confounding medication prior to Week 12; again, results of the primary and key secondary analyses were minimally impacted by the handling of this intercurrent event with composite versus treatment policy strategy.
L290: Although the coprimary endpoints were evaluated at Week 12, the double-blind treatment period continued through Week 24. It is of clinical interest to consider the comparison between remibrutinib 25 mg and placebo through the end of the double-blind period for this chronic condition. The review team used an MMRM analysis to estimate treatment differences at Week 24 and results are summarized in Table 100 and Table 101 of Section 16.1.1. For change from baseline in HSS7 at Week 24, the estimated differences in LS means relative to placebo were -2.91 (95% CI: -4.16, -1.66) and -3.61 (95% CI: -4.92, -2.29) in Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively. For change from baseline in ISS7 at Week 24, the estimated differences in LS means relative to placebo were -1.81 (95% CI: -2.88, -0.73) and -3.03 (95% CI: -4.16, -1.90) in Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively. Although the effect estimates are smaller at Week 24 than Week 12, both comparisons were nominally significant.
L291: To further examine the treatment effect over time, plots of the weekly mean change from baseline with standard error bars were produced. Figure 4 and Figure 5 depict the weekly mean change from baseline in HSS7 in Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively, through Week 52. Figure 6 and Figure 7 depict the weekly mean change from baseline in ISS7 in Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively, through Week 52. Both treatment groups in both trials experienced decreases in mean changes in HSS7 and ISS7 from baseline and there is a separation between the groups during the double-blind, placebo-controlled treatment period.

L304: ## 6.2.2.8.4. Key Secondary Efficacy Results, Trials A2301 and A2302
L305: Table 14 summarizes the results for the first key secondary efficacy endpoint of change from baseline to Week 12 in UAS7 for Trials A2301 and A2302. UAS7 is the sum of HSS7 and ISS7, and as such the results are closely related to the results for the coprimary endpoints. After adjusting for baseline UAS7 score, geographical region, prior exposure to anti-IgE biologics, visit week, treatment by visit interaction, and baseline UAS7 score by visit interaction, the estimated least-squares mean changes from baseline in UAS7 scores at Week 12 were greater in magnitude in the remibrutinib groups (LS mean =-20.02 (SE 0.72) and -19.41 (SE 0.70)) compared to placebo (LS mean =-13.79 (SE 0.98) and =-11.73 (SE 0.95)) in Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively. In Trials A2301 and A2302, the respective estimated differences in LS means relative to placebo were -6.22 (95% CI: -8.45, -4.00) and -7.68 (95% CI: -9.91, -5.46), which were statistically significant. The analyses which correspond to the estimand with treatment policy strategy for all intercurrent events were substantially similar, with p-values &lt;0.001 and small differences in point estimates and confidence intervals (at the most in the tenths place).
