L310: Table 15 summarizes the key secondary responder endpoints adjusted for geographical region, prior exposure to anti-IgE biologics, and baseline score, which were all statistically significant based on the prespecified testing procedure. For the responder endpoint of UAS7 ≤6 at Week 12, more subjects responded to remibrutinib (49.8% and 46.8%) than placebo (24.8% and 19.6%) with an estimated conditional odds ratio of 3.1 (95% CI: 2.0, 4.8) and 3.8 (95% CI: 2.4, 6.1) for Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively. For the responder endpoint of complete absence of itch and hives defined as UAS7 =0 at Week 12, more subjects responded to remibrutinib (31.1% and 27.9%) than placebo (10.5% and 6.5%) with an estimated conditional odds ratio of 3.8 (95% CI: 2.2, 6.8) and 5.8 (95% CI: 2.9, 11.6) in Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively. For these two responder analyses, the corresponding analyses for the estimand with treatment policy strategy for all intercurrent events were substantially similar, with p&lt;0.001 and small differences in point estimates and confidence intervals (at the most in the tenths place). For the responder endpoint of UAS7 ≤6 at Week 2, more subjects responded to remibrutinib (33.7% and 30.0%) than placebo (3.3% and 5.9%) with an estimated conditional odds ratio of 15.7 (95% CI: 6.3, 39.0) and 7.9 (95% CI: 3.8, 16.7) in Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively. The intercurrent event of intake of
L311: NDA 218436
L312: RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
L313: strongly confounding medication did not occur for any subjects prior to Week 2, and so the handling strategy for this intercurrent event does not impact this endpoint's analysis. For the responder endpoint of DLQI = 0 to 1 at Week 12, more subjects responded to remibrutinib (39.0% and 35.7%) than placebo (22.2% and 18.3%) with an estimated conditional odds ratio of 2.4 (95% CI: 1.5, 3.9) and 2.8 (95% CI: 1.7, 4.6) in Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively; however, this endpoint is not fit-for-purpose for CSU.

L319: **Table 16** summarizes the results for the remaining key secondary endpoints adjusted for geographical region, prior exposure to anti-IgE biologics, and an offset for subject's time in the treatment period up to Week 12, which were both statistically significant based on the prespecified testing procedure. The average cumulative number of weeks with UAS7 ≤6 up to Week 12 was longer for the remibrutinib group (5.2 and 4.5) compared to the placebo group (1.9 and 1.4), with an estimated rate ratio of 2.7 (95% CI: 2.0, 3.6) and 3.3 (95% CI: 2.3, 4.7) in Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively. The average cumulative number of angioedema-free weeks, defined as AAS7 =0, up to Week 12 was longer for the remibrutinib group (8.4 and 8.8) compared to the placebo group (6.7 and 6.7), with an estimated rate ratio of 1.3 (95% CI: 1.1, 1.4) and 1.3 (95% CI: 1.2, 1.5) in Trials A2301 and A2302, respectively. The analyses for these two endpoints which correspond to the estimand with treatment policy strategy for all intercurrent events were substantially similar, with p-value&lt;0.001 and minimal differences in point estimates and confidence intervals. While the cumulative weeks endpoints show numerical advantages for remibrutinib, these findings raise several uncertainties regarding their clinical meaningfulness and practical significance for informing prescribers. These endpoints do not address whether the periods of symptom control were consecutive or intermittent; for small treatment differences, intermittent control may be less impactful to patients. The Applicant proposed to include both endpoints in Section 14 of the USPI; however, the difference of approximately 3 weeks with UAS7 ≤6 between the treatment arms is not informative beyond the other efficacy claims to be included in the label, such as the proportion of subjects achieving UAS7 ≤6 at Weeks 2 and 12 and figures of ISS7 and HSS7 over time through Week 24. For angioedema-free weeks, the difference between remibrutinib and placebo over 12 weeks represents less than two additional weeks of freedom from angioedema symptoms. While statistically significant with a rate ratio of 1.3, this translates to less than two additional angioedema-free weeks over a 3-month period. See Section 6.3.1 for additional discussion.

L325: ## 6.2.2.8.5. Subgroup Analyses, Trials A2301 and A2302
L326: Results of subgroup analyses by demographics and baseline disease characteristics demonstrated similar treatment effects on the primary outcome compared to results in the overall study population, all favoring remibrutinib (Figure 69, Figure 70, Figure 71, and Figure 72). Although only a small subset of subjects had prior omalizumab exposure, an exploratory post hoc analysis showed a favorable treatment effect in this subgroup (Figure 73). See Sections 15.1.4.4, 15.1.4.6, 16.1.4, and 16.2.1 for additional details.
L327: ## 6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L328: ## 6.3.1. Evaluation of Weekly Angioedema Activity Score
L329: (b) (4)
L330: ## Issue
L331: The [REDACTED] (b) (4) cumulative number of angioedema-free weeks (defined as AAS7=0) from baseline to Week 12 in Trials A2301 and A2302.
L332: ## Background
L333: Angioedema is present in a substantial proportion of subjects with chronic spontaneous urticaria, affecting approximately 40 to 50% of cases, either in combination with wheals or rarely as the sole manifestation. In contrast to bradykinin mediated angioedema (e.g., hereditary angioedema), the angioedema in CSU is mast-cell mediated and typically shorter in duration (resolving within 24 to 72 hours). When present, angioedema associated with CSU generally responds to available therapies, but can be a more difficult to treat symptom in some patients. [REDACTED] (b) (4)
L334: (b) (4)
L335: ## Assessment
L336: The AAS is a patient-reported outcome instrument that quantifies angioedema activity over a specified period. The AAS7 refers to the sum of daily AAS scores over a 7-day period, analogous to the UAS7 used in urticaria. The AAS consists of five items, each scored daily (0 to 3 points per item, maximum 15 per day), with higher scores indicating greater disease activity. The total AAS7 is the sum of daily scores for seven consecutive days (range 0 to 105). See Section 15.1.3 for additional details on the content of the AAS7. Though the Division of Clinical Outcome Assessment was not formally consulted for evaluation of content validity of the AAS7, the review team generally considers the measurement tool to adequately cover relevant aspects of angioedema and that an AAS7=0 is inherently meaningful.
L337: However, the clinical significance of the difference in average cumulative number of angioedema-free weeks (AAS7=0) up to Week 12 between treatment groups is unclear. In the remibrutinib arm, the average number of angioedema free weeks was 8.43 compared to 6.72 weeks in the placebo arm in Trial A2301, and 8.81 weeks versus 6.68 weeks respectively in Trial
L338: A2302. Over 24 weeks, remibrutinib subjects had more angioedema-free weeks than placebo subjects (17.43 versus 14.45 weeks in Trial A2301; 18.03 versus 13.97 weeks in Trial A2302).
L339: Additionally, there were differences in the baseline angioedema burden between treatment groups across both pivotal studies. In Study A2301, the mean AAS7 score was 27.94 in the remibrutinib arm compared to 23.46 in the placebo arm, while in Study A2302, the remibrutinib arm had a mean AAS7 score of 25.18 versus 19.59 in the placebo arm. This imbalance in baseline favoring placebo added further uncertainty to the clinical interpretation of the results, particularly when considering the proportion of patients achieving angioedema-free status (AAS7 = 0) favored remibrutinib throughout the study by Week 24. In the remibrutinib group, 83.7% of subjects in Study A2301 were angioedema-free compared to 74.2% of subjects receiving placebo, while in Study A2302, the rates were 85.2% versus 72.1% respectively. Given the difference in baseline AAS7 scores, it cannot be discerned whether the increase in angioedema free weeks in the remibrutinib group was due to the effect of drug, regression to the mean, or baseline imbalance in angioedema symptoms.
L340: While remibrutinib appeared to demonstrate statistically significant superiority over placebo in reducing angioedema burden in subjects with CSU, the clinical significance of cumulative-free weeks of AAS7 = 0 is unclear and the difference in baseline angioedema burden between the treatment arms limited interpretability. Additionally, the numerical difference between the treatment groups is relatively small without obvious clinical significance.
L341: ## Conclusion
L342: Cumulative-free weeks of AAS7 = 0 is of unknown clinical significance. (b) (4)
L343: (b) (4)
L344: ## 7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
L345: ## 7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Nonclinical Data
L346: The nonclinical safety profile of remibrutinib supporting this NDA has been evaluated in a comprehensive program that included repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats and dogs, reproductive and developmental toxicity studies in rats and rabbits, and carcinogenicity studies in transgenic mice and in rats. In addition, pharmacokinetic and toxicokinetic studies were performed in dedicated studies in mice, rats, rabbits, and dogs and as part of the repeat-dose toxicity studies to support remibrutinib exposures and to compare pharmacokinetics and pharmacodynamics in animals and humans.
L347: Pharmacology studies show that remibrutinib inhibits human BTK in vitro with nanomolar potency and is more selective for BTK compared to the BTK-related enzymes, TEC and BMX. In a safety pharmacology battery of studies, remibrutinib had no effects in respiratory or central nervous systems. Remibrutinib inhibited human ether-à-go-go-related gene currents in vitro (by 15.6%, 42.6%, 69.6%, and 88.9% for 0.3\muM, 1\muM, 3\muM, and 10\muM remibrutinib, respectively). However, there were no cardiovascular effects observed in dogs at single doses up to 450 mg/kg/day remibrutinib. Pharmacokinetic studies were conducted in rats, mice, and dogs
L348: with single oral and/or intravenous dosing of remibrutinib. Single-dose studies demonstrated broad distribution of remibrutinib in all tissues, except for the brain, lens of the eye, spinal cord, and white fat. Remibrutinib had rapid absorption with a time to maximum plasma concentration (T_{max}) of 0.25 to 8 hours. Binding of remibrutinib to plasma proteins was high (&gt;90%) across all species. Remibrutinib was shown to be primarily eliminated via metabolism and excretion through the feces. No major metabolites were identified in humans.
L349: The most frequently observed findings in the repeat-dose toxicology studies were observed in the lymph nodes, thyroid glands, liver, pancreas, and blood. Similar findings have been observed with other BTK inhibitors for the liver, mesenteric lymph node, and pancreas along with hemorrhaging. Embryo-fetal toxicity occurred in pregnant animals administered BTK inhibitors during organogenesis.
L350: ## Lymph Nodes
L351: Decreased cellularity and germinal centers were observed in the mesenteric lymph nodes and gut-associated lymphoid tissue. In addition, sinusoidal red blood cells were observed in the mandibular and mesenteric lymph nodes in the 39-week dog study. Similar findings were also reported in the 26-week rat study. Sinusoidal red blood cells, sinusoidal dilatation, focal angiomatous hyperplasia, diffuse reticuloendothelial hypertrophy/hyperplasia, and/or pigmented macrophages were reported in the 2-year carcinogenicity study in rats.
L352: ## Thyroid Gland
L353: Follicular hypertrophy was observed in the 2-, 13-, and 26-week rat studies and in the 2-year carcinogenicity study in rats.
L354: ## Liver
L355: Minimal hepatocellular hypertrophy, multinucleated hepatocytes, pigment, mitosis, periportal vacuolation, and focal necrosis were observed accompanied by increased liver weights in the 26-week rat study.
L356: ## Pancreas
L357: Minimal islet fibrosis characterized by multilobulated, irregularly outlined islets with fibrosis/collagen/pigment was observed in the 26-week rat study and in the 2-year carcinogenicity study in rats. BTK inhibitors have been associated with rat-specific pancreatic lesions characterized by islet fibrosis, inflammation, and hemorrhage (Erickson et al. 2017).
L358: ## Blood
L359: Inhibition of collagen-induced platelet aggregation and prolonged bleeding time were observed in both rat and dog toxicology studies.

L367: ## Reproductive and Developmental Toxicity
L368: Fertility was unaffected in rats and fetal malformations were not observed in pregnant rats. However, in an embryo-fetal development study in pregnant rabbits administered up to 450 mg/kg/day remibrutinib during organogenesis, increases in the external malformations such as eye opening, small jaws, and hyperflexion of forelimbs were observed at doses 141 times the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) on an area under the concentration-time curve (AUC) basis. No increases in the incidence of malformations or variations were observed at doses that were 23 times the MRHD on an AUC basis. In a pre- and postnatal developmental toxicity study in rats, reduced litter, an increased number of stillborn pups, and an increased number of pup deaths were reported. No adverse remibrutinib-related changes were observed in maternal rats and offspring at doses that were 58 times the MRHD on a body surface area basis.
L369: ## 7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
L370: Remibrutinib is an oral kinase inhibitor, that primarily inhibits the activity of BTK, an enzyme involved in the signaling pathways of B-cells and other immune cells. These inhibitors have been evaluated in the treatment of hematologic malignancies and autoimmune diseases. A class effect directly due to BTK inhibition theoretically includes increased risk of bleeding in both the presence and absence of thrombocytopenia, suggesting an impaired platelet function as an underlying cause. Off-target effects such as atrial fibrillation, hypertension, ventricular arrhythmias, and increased risk of infection, including opportunistic infections, due to effects on both adaptive and innate immunity have been reported particularly in those receiving less selective BTK inhibitors for the treatment of hematologic malignancies and have been included in the Warnings and Precautions section of the label.
L371: ## 7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L372: Remibrutinib is not yet approved in the United States or any foreign market for any indication; therefore, no postmarketing experience is available for remibrutinib.
L373: ## 7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
L374: The clinical data scientists and clinical reviewer performed independent safety analyses using R Studio 4.1.3, JMP, and Analysis Studio (Office of Computational Science) software programs. All clinical studies conducted as part of the remibrutinib development program for CSU were evaluated for safety; however, primary safety analyses were conducted in the SAF population (total number of subjects [N]=912), defined as all subjects who received at least one dose of study treatment, regardless of randomization, during the 24-week controlled treatment period in Trials A2301 and A2302. Subjects were analyzed according to the study treatment received. Given the identical study designs and similar safety findings in the individual trials, pooled controlled safety data from Trials A2301 and A2302 are presented in this review. In addition, supportive safety data from the open-label, uncontrolled treatment periods of Trials A2301 and A2302, the phase 2 dose-finding Study A2201, the phase 2 open-label extension Study A2201E1, the phase 3 open-label, single-arm study in Japanese adult subjects with CSU, Study 1301, were independently analyzed and reviewed (see Section 17).
L375: The prespecified safety analysis plan and definitions were reviewed during development under the IND and were acceptable to the clinical review team.
L376: ## 7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
L377: The safety database is adequate to assess the safety of remibrutinib at the proposed dosage for the proposed indication. No data quality or integrity issues were identified in the submitted datasets or case report forms. Definitions of AEs and SAE were consistent with CFR 312.32. Treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were defined as AEs occurring from the first dose of study treatment through 4 weeks (28 days) after the last dose, or until the end of the study period, whichever came first. The Applicant determined AE severity according to the Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events (CTCAE) version 5.0 toxicity grading scale. Verbatim AEs were coded to standard AE terms using the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) version 26.1 for Trials A2301 and A2302. The Applicant's mapping of verbatim terms to MedDRA preferred terms for the events reported in the trial was reviewed and found to be acceptable.
L378: The primary safety database for remibrutinib at the proposed dosage of 25 mg BID consists of the 912 subjects treated in the 24-week, blinded, placebo-controlled, treatment periods in Trials A2301 and A2302. Additional support for the safety of remibrutinib, particularly for long-term use, is derived from the 28-week, uncontrolled, open-label treatment periods in Trials A2301 and A2302; a phase 2 randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, dose-ranging Study A2201 and its 52-week open-label extension Study A2201E1, and a 52-week, open-label study in adult Japanese subjects, Study A1301, all of which are discussed further in Sections 15 and 17.
L379: ## NDA 218436
## RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
L380: Note, the Applicant also submitted a multicenter, open-label phase 3 trial, Study A2305, to evaluate the effect of remibrutinib 25 mg BID on blood pressure in subjects with CSU inadequately controlled by second generation H1-antihistamine using a 24-hour blood pressure measurement by ambulatory blood pressure monitoring (ABPM) at steady state (Week 4) compared to baseline. Refer to the ABPM study report review in DARRTS dated June 13, 2025 (DARRTS reference ID 5608166) and Sections 15.5 and 17.6 of this review for additional details.
L381: The extent of remibrutinib exposure in Trials A2301 and A2302, as shown in Table 18, is adequate to support the safety of a chronically administered drug. During the entire study period for the pivotal trials, which extended up to 56 weeks including a 4-week follow-up period, the remibrutinib 25-mg BID group achieved a median exposure duration of 52.0 weeks with a cumulative exposure of 672 patient-years. Subjects who transitioned from placebo to remibrutinib at Week 24 had a median exposure duration of 28.1 weeks with 134.48 patient-years of cumulative exposure.

L395: ## 7.6.1.2. Deaths, Pooled Analyses, Trials A2301 and A2302
L396: There were no deaths reported during Trials A2301 and A2302, or in the entire clinical development program.
L397: ## 7.6.1.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Pooled Analyses, Trials A2301 and A2302
L398: The occurrence of SAEs during the 24-week placebo-controlled periods was relatively infrequent and mainly single events with no major imbalances between treatment groups. A total of 20 subjects (3.3%) in the remibrutinib 25-mg BID group experienced SAEs compared to 7 subjects (2.3%) in the placebo group. A subject-level listing of SAEs that occurred in the remibrutinib-treated arm are listed in Table 20. Based on a review of case narratives, most SAEs were related to underlying medical conditions or coincidental medical events and appear unrelated to remibrutinib.

L409: ## 7.6.1.4. Adverse Events and FDA Medical Queries Leading to Treatment Discontinuation, Pooled Analyses, Trials A2301 and A2302
L410: The analysis of AEs leading to treatment discontinuation is a useful indicator of the tolerability for chronic remibrutinib therapy for CSU. During the 24-week placebo-controlled period, overall, AEs leading to study treatment discontinuation were generally low and balanced between treatment groups. A total of 17 subjects (2.8%) in the remibrutinib 25-mg BID group discontinued treatment due to adverse events compared to 9 subjects (2.9%) in the placebo group. A subject-level listing of AEs that led to treatment discontinuation in the remibrutinib arm is shown in Table 21. Bleeding-related events, though rare, including petechiae, purpura, and contusion, were more commonly reported reasons for discontinuation in the remibrutinib group than in the placebo group (3 [0.5%] versus 0). Otherwise, there was no clear pattern of organ specific toxicity requiring discontinuation noted during the review with isolated AEs distributed across multiple organ systems.

L420: ## 7.6.1.5. Common Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Pooled Analyses, Trials A2301 and A2302
L421: The overall incidence of TEAEs was similar between treatment groups, with 64.9% of remibrutinib subjects (393/606) and 64.7% of placebo subjects (198/306) experiencing at least one TEAE as shown in Table 22. The severity distribution was predominantly mild-to-moderate across both groups, with severe adverse events occurring infrequently and in similar proportions (3.0% versus 2.3% for remibrutinib and placebo, respectively). Moderate adverse events were reported in 26.7% of remibrutinib subjects compared to 22.9% of placebo subjects, while mild events occurred in 35.1% and 39.5%, respectively.
L422: The most commonly reported TEAEs that occurred more frequently in the remibrutinib group compared to placebo are shown in the tables below by individual preferred terms (PTs) and by standardized groupings of PTs using OND custom medical queries (OCMQs).

L444: ## 7.6.1.6. Subgroup Analyses, Pooled Analyses, Trials A2301 and A2302
L445: In general, the AE profile across different demographic subgroups was balanced between treatment groups as shown in Table 106, Section 17.1. Differences in frequency of AEs between treatment arms was most prominent in the ≥75-year age group. However, the numbers of AEs were small and were consistent with normal age-related expectations. Although the majority of subjects were white or Asian making assessment of across all racial groups difficult, there were no clear trends indicating increased AE risk based on race. Likewise, the safety profile was consistent across sex, country, and ethnicity. See Section 17.1 for additional details.
L446: ## 7.6.1.7. Laboratory Findings, Pooled Analyses, Trials A2301 and A2302
L447: Laboratory evaluation in the remibrutinib clinical development program included assessment of hematology, clinical chemistry, and urinalysis parameters collected per the schedule of assessments (Table 98). During the 24-week placebo-controlled period, the majority of newly occurring laboratory abnormalities were Grade 1 or Grade 2 based on CTCAE criteria and were generally balanced between the remibrutinib and placebo groups. Grade 3 and Grade 4
L448: abnormalities were infrequent in both treatment groups, with no Grade 4 hematology events reported during the placebo-controlled period and rare Grade 3 events that were similarly distributed between groups. Laboratory findings related to liver function tests and cytopenias are discussed in Sections 7.6.1.7 and 7.7.3.
L449: The analysis of immunoglobulin levels revealed small decreases in median IgG and immunoglobulin M (IgM) levels from baseline in the remibrutinib group (-0.64 \text{ g/L} for IgG and -0.17 \text{ g/L} for IgM) compared to placebo (-0.45 \text{ g/L} for IgG and -0.05 \text{ g/L} for IgM); however, the clinical relevance of these changes is uncertain as values remained within normal ranges and decreased levels were not associated with increased infection rates (see Section 7.7.2) or other clinical findings.
L450: ## 7.6.1.8. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Pooled Analyses, Trials A2301 and A2302
L451: Because hepatotoxicity has been identified as a potential risk associated with other BTK inhibitors, the assessment of potential drug-induced liver injury was of particular interest in this application review. The exact mechanism of BTKi-induced liver injury is not fully understood and remains a relatively rare TEAE. Factors that may increase the risk of liver injury with BTK inhibitors include preexisting liver conditions, such as hepatic steatosis, and other medications. Additionally, certain BTK inhibitors have boxed warnings to highlight the risk of hepatotoxicity or list hepatotoxicity in the warnings and precautions section of the label.
L452: The hepatotoxicity assessment for remibrutinib included a review of labs (liver enzymes—ALT, AST, alkaline phosphatase, gamma-glutamyl transferase, and total bilirubin) and TEAEs with systematic evaluation using both CTCAE grading criteria and evaluation of drug-induced serious hepatotoxicity plots to identify potential Hy's Law cases.
L453: During the placebo-controlled period, liver enzyme abnormalities were generally balanced between treatment groups and primarily consisted of asymptomatic, isolated transaminase elevations without concurrent bilirubin increases (i.e., ALT and/or AST without concurrent increase of bilirubin). ALT elevations &gt;3\times ULN occurred in 1.2% of remibrutinib subjects versus 1.3% of placebo subjects, with most elevations falling between 3\times and &lt;5\times ULN as shown in Table 24.

L465: Two subjects in the remibrutinib group experienced ALT elevations &gt;5x ULN. The first case involved a 62-year-old female with peak ALT of 5.3x ULN at Week 12 in Trial A2301, occurring in the context of concurrent medications including amoxicillin/clavulanic acid and nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs, with rapid normalization following treatment discontinuation. The second case was a 46-year-old female with peak ALT of 14.6x ULN at Week 12, also associated with concurrent metamizole and prior amoxicillin/clavulanic acid use, showing rapid normalization after treatment interruption and successful negative rechallenge upon treatment resumption.
L466: In evaluation of drug-induced serious hepatotoxicity plot analyses, no potential Hy's Law cases (i.e., concurrent ALT or AST &gt;3x ULN and total bilirubin &gt;2x ULN) were identified in the SAF population during the 24-week controlled treatment period; likewise, there were no potential Hy's Law cases observed in the entire 52-week treatment periods of the A2301 and A2302 or in the supportive safety studies (see Section 17). Based on available data, the proposed dosage of remibrutinib does not appear to pose a significant risk for drug-induced livery injury and no additional monitoring beyond routine clinical care is required.
L467: ## 7.6.1.9. Vital Signs, Pooled Analyses, Trials A2301 and A2302
L468: Vital signs were captured according to the schedule of assessments (Table 98) at each study visit, approximately every 4 weeks. There was no clinically significant impact on vital signs with remibrutinib or major differences between treatment groups. In addition, the Applicant conducted a dedicated 24-hour ambulatory blood pressure monitoring study (A2305) in 144 subjects with CSU inadequately controlled by H1-antihistamines; the results showed no substantial change in blood pressure with remibrutinib treatment (25 mg BID) after 4 weeks. See Sections 15.6 and 17.6 and the Interdisciplinary Review Team consult memo dated June 13, 2025, for additional details regarding Study A2305.
L469: ## 7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
L470: ## 7.7.1. Bleeding Risk
L471: ## Issue
L472: Because of its mechanism of action, remibrutinib has the potential to cause bleeding.
L473: ## Background
L474: BTK inhibitors as a class, most notably ibrutinib, but also second-generation agents such as acalabrutinib and zanubrutinib, have been associated with an increased risk of bleeding. The bleeding risk may be due to BTK-dependent platelet dysfunction, which impairs platelet aggregation and increases susceptibility to both minor and, less commonly, major hemorrhagic events.
L475: ## Assessment
L476: The clinical review team consulted the Division of Nonmalignant Hematology to assist in the bleeding risk assessment for remibrutinib. Rather than the Applicant's custom query which included PTs for platelet function (platelet aggregation abnormal, platelet aggregation decreased, platelet aggregation inhibition, platelet dysfunction, platelet function test abnormal, and platelet toxicity), the Division of Nonmalignant Hematology recommended using the standardized MedDRA query or OCMQ for 'hemorrhage' as it only includes PTs for observed bleeding reactions. Although abnormal laboratory tests may correlate with increased bleeding risk, this relationship is not straightforward and varies depending on the specific test used, underlying conditions, severity of abnormalities, and other hemostatic factors. As such, the assessment of hemorrhagic adverse events should represent documented bleeding occurrences during trials, not laboratory markers of potential bleeding risk.
L477: An analysis of bleeding TEAEs using the OCMQ for 'hemorrhage' is shown in Table 25. In the pooled safety population, bleeding reactions occurred in 9% of subjects treated with remibrutinib compared to 2% in the placebo group during the 24-week controlled treatment period. Petechiae (4%) and contusion (2%) were the most commonly reported TEAEs. No severe bleeding reactions occurred. There were two bleeding events that were classified as serious, one occurred in relation to an accident and was considered serious due to hospitalization and the second event was hematuria that occurred in a subject with chronic urocystitis with squamous metaplasia. Neither event led to treatment discontinuation, and both resolved with further intervention. In the remibrutinib treatment arm, 0.5% experienced bleeding reactions that led to treatment discontinuation, while none of these reactions occurred in the placebo group. Exposure-adjusted occurrence rate (EAOR) for bleeding did not appear to increase over the entire 52-week study period compared to the placebo-controlled period, suggesting the risk did not increase with longer treatment duration and the first occurrence of bleeding events was highest within the first 4 to 12 weeks of treatment. Regarding dose-response relationships, the available data from higher doses is limited. In the phase 2b extension Study A2201E1, subjects received remibrutinib 100 mg BID (four times the proposed therapeutic dose), and one SAE of melena was reported, though this was attributed to concurrent bariatric surgery rather than drug effect (see Sections 17.1, 17.3, and 17.4 for additional details).

L490: To further explore the imbalance in bleeding TEAEs, the review team assessed whether there is an exposure-response relationship for this safety signal. A comparison of bleeding TEAE rates (using the Applicant's custom query) by quartiles of remibrutinib average concentrations at steady state (C_{\text{avg,ss}}) indicated no significant increase in bleeding events with increasing C_{\text{avg,ss}} quartile. The 95% CIs for the proportion of subjects with bleeding overlapped between the different exposure quartiles across three distinct safety data pools: E-R Safety Pool 1a, E-R Safety Pool 1b, and E-R Safety Pool 2 (Table 26). Additionally, no significant difference was observed in remibrutinib C_{\text{avg,ss}} between subjects who experienced bleeding and those who did not, across all three safety data pools (Figure 8). Furthermore, exposure-response analyses for safety indicated no significant relationship between the incidence of bleeding and increasing remibrutinib C_{\text{avg,ss}} in any of the safety data pools examined (Figure 9).

L503: Source: Exposure-response analysis for efficacy and safety of remibrutinib in adult subjects with Chronic Spontaneous Urticaria—Modeling Report Figure 7-24.
L504: Abbreviations: AESI, adverse event of special interest; bid, twice daily; CI, confidence interval; E-R, exposure-response; qd, once daily; SS, steady state
L505: Notably, there are limitations to the above exposure-response analyses for safety. For example, in Safety Pool 1a and Safety Pool 1b, both of which consider data from Trials A2301 and A2302, only a single remibrutinib dosage of 25 mg BID was evaluated. Although Safety Pool 2 also included data from dose-ranging Study A2201, which assessed dosages up to 100 mg BID, data are only considered for the 12-week placebo-controlled period. Thus, these analyses do not sufficiently inform the potential bleeding risk following chronic exposure to higher doses/exposures of remibrutinib.
L506: In the proposed USPI, the Applicant recommends withholding remibrutinib for 3 to 7 days pre- and postsurgery. However, with a half-life of only 1 to 2 hours and time to maximum concentration of approximately 1 hour, the drug would be nearly completely eliminated within 5 to 10 hours (five to six half-lives). The Applicant provided justification for the interruption period in a September 4, 2025 response to a labeling information request, stating that the 3-day presurgical interruption accounts for the 48 to 72-hour platelet function recovery time following covalent BTK inhibition. The 7-day consideration for postsurgical reintroduction is primarily based on contemporary surgical literature recommendations for resuming treatment after major surgical interventions, allowing adequate time for initial wound healing and hemostatic stability.
L507: Regarding coadministration of remibrutinib with anticoagulants, no clinical data exists because concomitant use was prohibited in the phase 3 clinical trials. Limited data were available for antiplatelet agents (aspirin up to 100 mg daily and clopidogrel up to 75 mg daily were permitted); however, the use of dual antiplatelet therapy, such as the combination of acetylsalicylic acid and clopidogrel, was prohibited.
L508: ## Conclusion
L509: Remibrutinib is associated with an increased risk of bleeding reactions, likely due to its mechanism of action and impact on platelet function; however, most events were related to bruising/petechiae/contusion rather than overt hemorrhage. Reported bleeding TEAEs were primarily mild or moderate in severity, with few requiring medical intervention or treatment discontinuation. Supportive safety data suggest that there is no increase in risk over time with extended use or higher doses; however, these data are limited. Given that CSU is a symptomatic and self-limited condition with multiple available treatment options, the review team recommends adding a Warning and Precaution to Section 5 of the USPI to inform prescribers of the potential risk of bleeding reactions and need for consideration of the benefit/risk for continued treatment if reactions occur. Given the observed safety signal for bleeding risk, withholding remibrutinib perioperatively is a reasonable recommendation and the review team accepted the Applicant's justification. The team recommends advising prescribers to consider the risks and benefits of concomitant antithrombotic use in Section 7 of the USPI given the absence of clinical data from phase 3 trials.
L510: ## 7.7.2. Infection Risk
L511: ## Issue
L512: Because of its mechanism of action, remibrutinib has the potential to cause immune compromise leading to increased infection.
L513: ## **Background**
L514: BTK is a cytoplasmic tyrosine kinase and member of the TEC kinase family, selectively expressed in cells of the adaptive and innate immune system, including B-cells, macrophages, mast cells, basophils, and thrombocytes. In addition to its role for signaling through the Fc\epsilon RI for IgE and the activating Fc\gamma R for IgG, BTK plays an important role in signaling through the B-cell receptor pathway, theoretically affecting primary IgM and IgG responses. Clinical experience with other BTK inhibitors revealed an increased risk for infections.
L515: ## **Assessment**
L516: During the 24-week placebo-controlled periods in Trials A2301 and A2302, the overall infection rates (based on MedDRA system organ class 'Infections and Infestations') were comparable between the remibrutinib 25-mg BID and placebo groups, with 33% versus 34% of subjects experiencing infections, respectively. There was no difference between treatment groups in the frequency of OCMQ terms for 'viral infection' or 'bacterial infection'. The exposure-adjusted incidence rates were similarly balanced at 95.6 versus 98.1 per 100 patient-years in the remibrutinib and placebo groups, respectively. The types of infections observed were consistent with those expected in the general population, predominantly consisting of respiratory tract infections including coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) (10.7% versus 11.4%), nasopharyngitis (6.6% versus 4.6%), and upper respiratory tract infections (3.0% versus 2.0%) in the remibrutinib versus placebo treatment arms, respectively. Viral infections were the most frequent, while bacterial and fungal infections were reported infrequently. No opportunistic infections, as might be expected with the use of a BTK inhibitor, such as invasive aspergillosis, cryptococcosis, or *Pneumocystis jirovecii* pneumonia nor encapsulated bacterial infections were reported in the remibrutinib group.
L517: The severity profile of infections demonstrated that most cases of infections were categorized as mild-to-moderate in nature (67 to 68% mild, 32% moderate). Serious infections were infrequent and occurred at comparable rates between treatment groups (0.8% versus 0.7%). There were two subjects in the remibrutinib treatment arm who experienced a severe infection, bronchitis and acrodermatitis, both moderate that did not lead to treatment interruption or discontinuation. Treatment modifications due to infections were minimal, with only one infection leading to study treatment discontinuation (COVID-19) and treatment interruptions occurring at similar rates between groups (8.4% versus 7.9% of events). Long-term safety data extending up to Week 52 showed no increase in infection rates with prolonged exposure. The exposure-adjusted occurrence rate decreased from 111.7 to 82.7 per 100 patient-years during the entire study period, suggesting no cumulative infection risk with extended treatment duration (see Section 17.1).
L518: From an immunological perspective, while small decreases in IgG and IgM levels were observed with remibrutinib treatment, these remained within normal ranges and showed no temporal association with infection occurrence. Similarly, no correlation was identified between cytopenia events and increased infection risk, further supporting the absence of clinically meaningful immunosuppression.
L519: The clinical trial data demonstrate that the infection risk with remibrutinib is comparable to placebo with no evidence of opportunistic/atypical infections or increase in frequency or severity of infections attributable to the drug. There was no change to the risk of infection with long-term use up to 1 year (52 weeks).
L520: ## Conclusion
L521: Despite its mechanism of action and known BTKi class effects for increasing susceptibility to infection, remibrutinib 25 mg BID does not appear to increase infection risk in subjects with CSU.
L522: ## 7.7.3. Cytopenias
L523: ## Issue
L524: Because of its mechanism of action, remibrutinib has the potential to cause cytopenias.
L525: ## Background
L526: BTK inhibitors are associated with an increased risk of cytopenias due to the pharmacological effect on B-cell signaling. BTK inhibitors, particularly less selective agents, may also have off-target effects on hematopoietic progenitors. The incidence and severity of these cytopenias vary by agent and clinical context, such as concomitant use with other cytotoxic agents or chemoimmunotherapy.
L527: ## Assessment
L528: Cytopenia was captured as an AESI for remibrutinib and reported using the standardized MedDRA query 'Haematopoietic cytopenias (broad).' The evaluation focused on monitoring for decreases in blood cell counts, particularly neutrophils, lymphocytes, and platelets, and assessing any potential clinical consequences such as increased infection risk or bleeding events. During the 24-week placebo-controlled period in the pivotal trials, cytopenia AESIs occurred infrequently in both the remibrutinib 25-mg BID group (22, 3.6%) and the placebo group (6, 2.0%) as shown in Table 27.
L529: **Table 27. Cytopenia AESIs by SMQ, SAF, Trials A2301 and A2302 (Pooled)**

L532: Source: Clinical Reviewer-generated using ADAE.xpt and ADSL.xpt in Analysis Studio
L533: Abbreviations: AESI, adverse event of special interest; CI, confidence interval; MedDRA, Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities; N, number of subjects; n, number of subjects meeting criteria; Nw, number of subjects with data; SAF, safety analysis set; SMQ, standardized MedDRA query
L534: All cytopenia AESIs were mild (75.0% versus 100.0%) or moderate (25.0% versus 0%) in severity, with no severe events reported. There were no SAEs related to cytopenia. The median time-to-event onset was 70.0 days in the remibrutinib group versus 35.0 days in the placebo group, with median event durations of 26.0 and 28.0 days, respectively. Most events resolved (90.6% versus 88.9%) while continuing the drug, and only 31.3% of events in the remibrutinib group were considered related to study treatment. No action was taken with the study treatment for most events (84.4%), with study treatment interrupted for only three events (9.4%) and discontinued for one event (3.1%) in the remibrutinib group. The difference between treatment groups was primarily driven by TEAEs for neutropenia (1.2% versus 0.3%) and neutrophil count decreased (0.3% versus 0%) in the remibrutinib and placebo groups, respectively. Grade 3 neutropenia, defined as an absolute neutrophil count of 500 to &lt;1000/mm3, occurred in two subjects in the remibrutinib group compared to one subject in the placebo group, neither of which was associated with a clinical infection. One occurred in a 42-year-old female at Week 16 who remained on treatment. She recovered by Week 20 but then worsened to Grade 1 (absolute neutrophil count between lower limit of normal and 1500/mm3). The second occurred in a 69-year-old female at Week 8 who, after interrupting treatment for 8 days, improved to Grade 1 at Week 12.
L535: During the placebo-controlled period, thrombocytopenia was reported in 3 patients (0.5%) in the remibrutinib treatment arm compared to 1 patient (0.3%) in the placebo group. All reported thrombocytopenia events were mild or moderate in severity, with no severe cases documented. One notable case involved a 21-year-old male patient who had a Grade 1 low platelet count at baseline and Week 2 that initially recovered to normal levels, but then developed a Grade 3 low platelet count of 39 × 109/L at Week 24. Importantly, this patient's platelet count showed normal values when retested just 2 days later, suggesting a transient laboratory finding rather than sustained thrombocytopenia. Another case involved a 29-year-old female patient who was initially reported to have a Grade 4 platelet decrease, but this was later determined to be due to incorrect reporting of laboratory parameter units by the local laboratory, with no clinically relevant platelet decrease actually occurring. There was no temporal association between low platelet counts and bleeding AESIs in either treatment group. The infrequent low platelet counts, and increased prothrombin INR values were not temporally associated with bleeding events, and the analysis revealed no correlation between bleeding AESIs and notable decreases in platelet counts.
L536: During the 52-week study periods, the EAOR did not increase (8.9 per 100 patient-years) compared to the placebo-controlled period (12.1 per 100 patient-years), suggesting no increase in cytopenia AESIs with longer treatment duration (see Section 17.1 for additional details).
L537: In addition to cytopenia AESIs, laboratory data were also analyzed because not all lab abnormalities were captured as TEAEs by investigators. Laboratory analysis revealed minimal shifts in blood cell counts, with a downward shift in median neutrophil counts (from 4.00 \times 10^9 cells/L at baseline to 3.75 \times 10^9 cells/L at Week 2) and platelet counts (from 272 \times 10^9 cells/L to 243 \times 10^9 cells/L at Week 2) observed in the remibrutinib group. However, these levels remained stable throughout treatment and within normal ranges, returning to baseline levels upon treatment cessation. Grade 3 and Grade 4 hematology abnormalities were infrequent and generally balanced between treatment groups, with no additional events identified beyond those that were captured as AESIs. There was no association between cytopenias and clinical bleeding events or infections.
L538: ## **Conclusion**
L539: Cytopenia occurred infrequently in the trial with small numerical differences between treatment groups. Although remibrutinib-treated subjects were more likely to report neutropenia or thrombocytopenia, the observed imbalances do not appear clinically significant.
L540: ## 7.7.4. Vaccine Immune Response
L541: ## **Issue**
L542: Because of its mechanism of action, remibrutinib may impact the immune response to vaccinations.
L543: (b) (4)
L544: ## **Background**
L545: The mechanism of action of BTK inhibitors, blockage of B-cell receptor and myeloid fragment crystallizable receptor mediated signaling with resultant decreased B-cell activation, antibody class-switching, expansion, and cytokine production would be expected to impact the immunogenicity of vaccines. By decreasing antigen presentation to T-cells, BTK inhibitors may lead to altered T-cell responses and interferon induction by vaccination. To evaluate the impact of remibrutinib on vaccine response, the Applicant conducted an immunogenicity study in healthy adults. In consultation with CBER, the Agency provided feedback on the study (written responses dated July 19, 2022) outlining key concerns such as whether a 1-week interruption of remibrutinib would be sufficient for immune system recovery, the appropriateness of conducting the study in healthy volunteers rather than the intended subject population, and the selection of immunogenicity endpoints. The Agency emphasized that immunogenicity studies are inherently difficult to interpret (b) (4), cautioning that vaccine responses cannot serve as surrogates for overall immune competence.
L546: ## **Assessment**
L547: Study F12101 was a double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel-group study to evaluate the impact of concomitant or interrupted remibrutinib treatment regimens in comparison with placebo on the response to three vaccines in healthy subjects. A total of 107 subjects were randomized in a 1:1:1 ratio to one of the three treatment arms: concomitant remibrutinib treatment, interrupted remibrutinib treatment, or placebo. Subjects were administered three different vaccines: a T-cell-dependent vaccine (quadrivalent seasonal influenza [Influsplit Tetra/Fluarix Tetra, GSK]), a T-cell-independent 23-valent pneumococcal polysaccharide
L548: NDA 218436
RHAPSIDO (remibrutinib, LOU064)
L549: vaccine (PPV-23, Pneumovax 23), and a T-cell-dependent neoantigen (keyhole Limpet hemocyanin [KLH]), on Day 15. See Section 15.6 for a detailed description of the study design.
L550: In consultation with CBER/Office of Vaccines Research and Review, the review team concluded that the results of the vaccine immune response study were not relevant (b) (4) for various reasons. Primarily, the study failed to employ the immunogenicity evaluations that CBER had previously accepted for determining vaccine effectiveness. Specifically, it did not measure geometric mean titers and proper seroconversion rates for influenza vaccines, nor did it use the opsonophagocytic antibody assay for pneumococcal vaccines, despite CBER providing guidance on these requirements in the 2022 Type C meeting responses to the Applicant. Instead, the study used less appropriate endpoints including IgG antibody levels for pneumococcal assessment, which are insufficient (b) (4).
L551: The inclusion of KLH as a study component further limited the study's regulatory relevance. CBER noted that KLH is not an FDA-licensed vaccine and that antibody responses to KLH lack established clinical significance for predicting responses to licensed vaccines. Finally, the study was conducted in healthy volunteers rather than the intended CSU patient population, limiting extrapolation of results.
L552: Regarding avoidance of live or live-attenuated vaccines, with input from CBER, the review team agreed that such recommendations would be appropriate for inclusion in Sections 5 (Warnings and Precautions) and 17 (Patient Counseling Information) of the USPI. Although not routinely included in labeling for other BTKi, recommendations to avoid live/live-attenuated vaccines have similarly been included in labeling for other immunomodulators (in the absence of clinical data), and patients with CSU are more likely to be candidates for live virus vaccines compared to the immunocompromised oncology populations typically treated with approved BTK inhibitors. For more details, refer to the CBER consult memo (Inter-Center Consult No.: 01066955) dated June 2, 2025, in the Document Archiving, Reporting, and Regulatory Tracking System (DARRTS) (Reference ID 5601342).
L553: ## Conclusion
L554: Given the lack of clinical data on the effects of live or live-attenuated vaccines in subjects receiving remibrutinib, the review team concurs with adding a Warning and Precaution to avoid use of live and live-attenuated vaccines.
L555: ## 7.7.5. Use in Subjects With Hepatic Impairment
L556: ## Issue
L557: (b) (4) patients with hepatic impairment (HI).
L558: ## Background
L559: Unlike drugs that can be dose-adjusted to accommodate patient-specific factors or drug interactions, the therapeutic window and safety profile of remibrutinib do not permit dose reductions or increases to compensate for altered drug exposure as only a single dose of remibrutinib (i.e., 25 mg BID) was evaluated in the pivotal phase 3 trials A2301 and A2302. As
L560: a result, there are limited clinical data to guide dose adjustments for patients with HI who experience 2- to 3.5-fold increases in drug exposure across all degrees of liver dysfunction (mild, moderate, and severe).
L561: ## **Assessment**
L562: The Applicant conducted a dedicated HI study, Study A2101, in which the pharmacokinetics of remibrutinib following administration of 25 mg BID was evaluated in subjects with mild (Child-Pugh Class A), moderate (Child-Pugh Class B), and severe (Child-Pugh Class C) HI compared to subjects with normal hepatic function matched for gender, age, and body weight. Following the administration of 25-mg BID remibrutinib, the geometric mean ratios for subjects with mild, moderate, and severe HI compared to subjects with normal hepatic function were 1.85, 1.65, and 1.99 for maximum plasma concentration (C_{max}), respectively, and 2.33, 2.3, and 3.49 for AUC_{last,ss}, respectively (Figure 10, Figure 35). For additional details, refer to Section 14.2.5.
L563: **Figure 10. Box Plots of C_{max} and AUC_{tau} in Subjects With Mild, Moderate, and Severe Hepatic Impairment vs. Healthy Controls**
L564: Source: Reviewer's Analysis based on ADPP.xpt dataset for Study A2101
L565: Abbreviations: AUC_{tau}, area under the concentration-time curve over the dosing interval; C_{max}, maximum plasma concentration; HI, hepatic impairment
L566: (b) (4) However, safety data
L567: from healthy subjects is not considered appropriate to reflect the safety in subjects with CSU. In addition, the safety data in subjects with CSU derived from Studies A2201 and A2201E1 are limited by the small number of subjects exposed for a short duration in the controlled Study A2201 and the inherent limitations of uncontrolled, unblinded data in Study A2201E1. Thus, exposure-response analyses for safety are not adequate to determine that chronic exposure to higher doses/exposures of remibrutinib is safe.
L568: Furthermore, CSU is not a life-threatening disease, and other approved therapies are available for the treatment of CSU, including in patients with HI. Given the potential risks associated with remibrutinib (e.g., bleeding events) and the lack of robust safety data with remibrutinib exposures 2.3- to 3.5-fold greater than that of the recommended dosage, the benefit-risk of exposing CSU patients with HI to higher exposures of remibrutinib is not favorable.
L569: ## Conclusion
L570: Given the limitations in assessing safety following administration of higher doses of remibrutinib, the use of remibrutinib in subjects with any degree of HI (mild, moderate, or severe) should be avoided. Consideration of the condition and benefit/risk assessment led to conservative dose-adjustment recommendations, favoring contraindications rather than dose modifications when adequate safety data were unavailable to support exposure changes.
L571: ## 7.7.6. Concomitant Use With CYP3A4 Modulators
L572: ## Issue
L573: (b) (4) concomitant use of strong or moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors.
L574: ## Background
L575: Unlike drugs that can be dose-adjusted to accommodate patient-specific factors or drug interactions, the therapeutic window and safety profile of remibrutinib do not permit dose reductions or increases to compensate for altered drug exposure as only a single dose of remibrutinib (i.e., 25 mg BID) was evaluated in the pivotal phase 3 Trials A2301 and A2302. The drug's susceptibility to CYP3A4-mediated interactions creates scenarios where dose adjustments would theoretically be beneficial but are not available as management strategies because there are limited clinical data to guide dose adjustments.
L576: Strong CYP3A4 inhibitors increase remibrutinib exposure by 3- to 4-fold, while strong inducers decrease exposure by 74% to 78%, yet there are no established dose-reduction or increase protocols to maintain therapeutic levels during these interactions.
L577: ## Assessment
L578: ## Strong CYP3A4 Inhibition
L579: Study X2103 evaluated the impact of multiple oral doses of ritonavir 100 mg, a strong CYP3A4/P-glycoprotein (P-gp) inhibitor, or grapefruit juice, an intestinal CYP3A4 inhibitor, on the pharmacokinetics of remibrutinib following administration of 20-mg remibrutinib capsules once daily in healthy subjects (see Section 14.2.6.1). Initially, remibrutinib 50 mg once daily was administered before evaluating the drug-drug interactions (DDIs) with ritonavir and grapefruit juice in order to achieve near-complete BTK receptor occupancy and steady-state conditions, thereby minimizing the influence of target-mediated drug disposition (TMDD) on remibrutinib PK measurements. Given the short apparent half-life of remibrutinib, it is not expected that administration of higher doses (i.e., 50 mg) will influence the pharmacokinetics following administration of the 20-mg dose used to evaluate the impact of CYP3A4 inhibition. The coadministration of ritonavir with remibrutinib 20 mg resulted in a 3.32-, 4.5-, and 4.31-fold increase in C_{max}, AUC_{last} and AUC_{0-inf} of remibrutinib, respectively (see Section 14.2.6.1).
L580: The coadministration of grapefruit juice with remibrutinib 20 mg resulted in a 1.24-, 1.33- and 1.28-fold increase in C_{max}, AUC_{last} and AUC_{0-inf} of remibrutinib, respectively, indicating a minimal intestinal first pass-effect on remibrutinib.
L581: Physiologically based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) simulations predict a 2.67- and 3.65-fold increase in C_{max,ss} and AUC_{tau}, respectively, of remibrutinib 25 mg BID following the oral administration of ritonavir (100 mg BID for 7 days) in healthy subjects. Additional PBPK simulations with other strong CYP3A4 inhibitors predict a 2.47- and 3.21-fold increase in C_{max,ss} and AUC_{tau} of remibrutinib 25 mg BID following the oral administration of itraconazole (200 mg QD for 7 days), and a 2.53- and 3.38-fold increase in C_{max,ss} and AUC_{tau} of remibrutinib 25 mg BID following the oral administration of ketoconazole (200 mg BID for 7 days) in healthy subjects (see Section 14.5.3).
L582: ## Moderate CYP3A4 Inhibition
L583: PBPK simulations predict a 1.9- and 2.27-fold increase in C_{max,ss} and AUC_{tau}, respectively, of remibrutinib 25 mg BID following the oral administration of erythromycin (500 mg QD for 7 days) in healthy subjects. Fluconazole, a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor, administered at 200 mg QD for 7 days was predicted to increase the C_{max,ss} and AUC_{tau} of remibrutinib 25 mg BID by 1.67- and 1.89-fold, respectively (see Section 14.5.3).
L584: ## Weak CYP3A4 Inhibition
L585: PBPK simulations predict a 1.04-fold increase in both C_{max,ss} and AUC_{tau} of remibrutinib 25 mg BID following the oral administration of fluvoxamine (50 mg QD for 7 days) in healthy subjects.
L586: ## CYP3A4 Inducers
L587: Study A02103 investigated the impact of carbamazepine (a strong CYP3A4 inducer) at a dosage of 300 mg BID on the exposure to remibrutinib 100 mg BID. Carbamazepine significantly decreased the C_{max,ss}, AUC_{tau}, and AUC_{last,ss} of remibrutinib 100 mg by 74%, 77%, and 78%, respectively. Refer to Section 14.2.6.2 for additional details.
L588: PBPK simulations predict a 64% and 74% reduction in the C_{max} and AUC_{tau}, respectively, of remibrutinib 25 BID when coadministered with carbamazepine compared to administration of remibrutinib alone. In addition, rifampicin (a strong CYP3A4 inducer) administered at 600 mg QD was predicted to decrease the C_{max,ss} and AUC_{tau} of remibrutinib 25 mg BID by 88% and 91%, respectively, compared to administration of remibrutinib alone (see Section 14.5.3). Efavirenz (a moderate CYP3A4 inducer) administered at 600 mg QD for 7 days was predicted to decrease the C_{max,ss} and AUC_{tau} of remibrutinib 25 BID by 60% and 64%, respectively (see Section 14.5.3). Dexamethasone (a weak CYP3A4 inducer) administered at 8 mg BID for 7 days was predicted to decrease the C_{max,ss} and AUC_{tau} of remibrutinib 25 BID by 33% and 38%, respectively (see Section 14.5.3).
L589: Overall, the data indicate that strong or moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors significantly increase the exposure to remibrutinib 25 mg BID.
L590: (b) (4)
(b) (4)
(b) (4) the benefit-risk of exposing patients with CSU to higher exposures of remibrutinib following coadministration with strong or moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors is not favorable.
L591: ## Conclusion
L592: The use of remibrutinib with strong or moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors should be avoided, and the USPI will be revised accordingly. Given the large decreases in remibrutinib exposure and the potential for loss of efficacy, coadministration of remibrutinib with moderate or strong CYP3A inducers should be avoided.
L593: ## 8. Therapeutic Individualization
L594: The dose-adjustment recommendations for remibrutinib are based on a dataset encompassing population pharmacokinetic analyses, dedicated special population studies, and drug-interaction trials across diverse patient populations. Population pharmacokinetic modeling was conducted using data from 1152 subjects across multiple clinical trials, providing the foundation for evaluating intrinsic factors including age (18 to 80 years), sex (63.5% females, 36.5% males), body weight (39 to 162 kg), and race/ethnicity distributions. For renal impairment evaluation, the dataset comprised 904 subjects (78.5%) with normal renal function (creatinine clearance \ge 90 mL/min), 222 subjects (19.3%) with mild impairment (60 to 90 mL/min), 25 subjects (2.2%) with mild-to-moderate impairment (45 to 60 mL/min), and one subject with severe impairment (15 to 30 mL/min), supplemented by mass balance Study X2104 data showing minimal renal elimination of unchanged drug (0.65% of oral dose).
L595: Hepatic impairment assessment relied on dedicated Study A2101, which evaluated remibrutinib 25 mg BID in subjects with mild (Child-Pugh Class A), moderate (Child-Pugh Class B), and severe (Child-Pugh Class C) hepatic impairment compared to matched healthy controls. Drug-interaction data were derived from multiple clinical studies, including Study X2103 evaluating ritonavir (strong CYP3A4 inhibitor) and grapefruit juice effects, Study A02103 assessing carbamazepine (strong CYP3A4 inducer) impact, and Study X2102 examining effects on CYP3A4, CYP2C9, and CYP1A2 substrates using probe drugs midazolam, tolbutamide, and caffeine. Additional interaction studies evaluated P-gp and breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP) substrate effects using digoxin and rosuvastatin as probe drugs. Food-effect data came from Study A02104, while acid-reducing agent interactions were assessed through pooled analysis of concomitant medication use in Trials A2201, A2301, and A2302.
L596: The safety database supporting dose-adjustment decisions included exposure-response analyses from three safety pools: E-R Safety Pool 1a (24-week placebo-controlled data from Trials A2301 and A2302), E-R Safety Pool 1b (entire treatment period from Trials A2301 and A2302), and E-R Safety Pool 2 (12-week data from Trials A2201, A2301, and A2302). However, the adequacy of safety data for higher exposures was limited, with the review team noting insufficient data from healthy subjects receiving up to 600 mg once daily and limited controlled data from subjects with CSU receiving 100 mg BID in Studies A2201 and A2201E1. Thus, exposure-response analyses for safety are not appropriate to inform on safety following chronic exposure to higher doses/exposures of remibrutinib. PBPK modeling supplemented clinical data to predict interactions with various CYP3A4 modulators not directly studied, providing additional support for drug-interaction recommendations.
L597: ## 8.1. Intrinsic Factors
L598: ## 8.1.1. Age
L599: No dosage adjustment is required based on age.
L600: Population pharmacokinetic (popPK) analyses indicated that age (range 18 to 80 years) does not significantly impact the pharmacokinetics of remibrutinib in subjects with CSU.
L601: ## 8.1.2. Body Weight
L602: No dosage adjustment is required based on body weight.
L603: PopPK modeling demonstrated a strong positive correlation between fat-free mass (FFM) and fat mass (FM) with body weight. The final population PK model indicated that FFM and FM were significant covariates affecting remibrutinib clearance, while only FM was a significant covariate affecting volume of distribution. An increase in FM is expected to elevate the exposure to remibrutinib, which is moderately lipophilic. Given that body weight is composed of both FFM and FM, the impact of body weight on the pharmacokinetics of remibrutinib was assessed through the combined effects of FFM and FM. This analysis estimates a 20% increase in area under the AUC for subjects with CSU with a total body weight of 49 kg (5th percentile of the overall dataset) and a 20% decrease in AUC for subjects with CSU with a total body weight of 109 kg (95th percentile of the overall dataset) relative to a typical patient with body weight of 70 kg. Overall, the impact of body weight (range 39 to 162 kg) on remibrutinib exposure is not considered clinically relevant.
L604: ## 8.1.3. Sex
L605: No dosage adjustment is required based on sex.
L606: The population PK results indicated a disparity in the distributions of FFM and FM between males and females, primarily due to the lower FFM and higher FM in females compared to males (Figure 11). After accounting for body weight, there was no notable effect of sex (63.5% females and 36.5% males) on the pharmacokinetics of remibrutinib in subjects with CSU.
L607: **Figure 11. Distribution of FFM and FM by Sex**
L608: Source: Population pharmacokinetics of remibrutinib to support labeled dosage in adult subjects with chronic spontaneous urticaria, Figure 7-5
Abbreviations: FFM, fat-free mass; FM, fat mass
L609: ## 8.1.4. Race/Ethnicity
L610: No dosage adjustment is required based on race/ethnicity.
L611: The observed exposure to remibrutinib by race measured 45 minutes following the administration of remibrutinib at 25 mg BID at Week 12 in phase 3 Trials A2301 and A2302 combined is provided in Figure 12. The postdose concentration of remibrutinib is comparable between the White (54.2%, N=265), Black (2.8%, N=14), Chinese (12.6%, N=62), and Asian non-Chinese (i.e., Indian (13.3%, N=65), Vietnamese (1.6%, N=8), and Korean (4%, N=20)) populations. Although there was a higher median concentration observed in the Japanese population (3.8%, N=19), it is within the range of variability of PK observations in the other populations. In addition, the proportion of the observed dataset that fell within the Japanese population was relatively small as compared to other race/ethnicity groups. Thus, the finding of greater median exposure among Japanese subjects should be interpreted with caution.
L612: The overall dataset for popPK analysis was comprised of 59.3% (N=683) non-Asian (i.e., White, Black, and Others) subjects, 8.8% (N=101) Mainland Chinese subjects, 12.2% (N=141) Japanese subjects, and 19.7% (N=227) other Asian (i.e., East Asian, no Mainland Chinese, Japanese; and Asian, no East Asian) subjects. In popPK analysis, the race/ethnicity groups of Mainland Chinese, Japanese, and Other Asian were identified as significant covariates affecting the clearance of remibrutinib. All race/ethnicity groups were determined to have lower clearance relative to the reference population of non-Asian. Consequently, popPK simulations indicate that remibrutinib AUC increases by 21% in Mainland Chinese subjects, 20% in Japanese subjects, and 30% in Other Asian subjects compared to non-Asian subjects, all at the same body weight of 70 kg (Figure 13).
L613: Overall, these differences in exposure are not considered to be clinically relevant.
L614: **Figure 12.** Observed Remibrutinib Concentration at 45 Minutes Following the Administration of Remibrutinib 25 mg BID at Week 12 in Phase 3, Trials A2301 and A2302
L615: Source: Reviewer's analysis based on PC.xpt and ADSL.xpt datasets for Trials A2301 and A2302
Asian population includes, 62 Chinese, 65 Indian, 19 Japanese, 20 Korean, and 8 Vietnamese subjects. The White population includes 265 subjects and the Black population includes 14 subjects.
Abbreviation: BID, twice daily

L617: Source: Population pharmacokinetics of remibrutinib to support labeled dosage in adult subjects with chronic spontaneous urticaria, Figure 7-18
